\boldsymbol{r}

Position in space

\boldsymbol{\theta}

Location on receiver sky

\rho

Arbitrary basic variable

In this work, an observation
{\mathsf{o}}
is the lowest level window for which independent data are analyzed: each distinct combination of angular resolution element, channel, and time yields a separate measurement and can count as an observation. Other useful windows are the survey
{\mathsf{s}}
, a complete collection of observations; and
{\mathsf{h}}{(t)}
, picking all objects in past history to the present as viewed from Earth. Still more are listed in Table
3
.
19
19

19

Although not used in this work, windows can be random. These would be a point process on their own “haystack.” In fact,
Wright et al. [188]
basically describe a survey window haystack
[
47
, see also]
.

A collection
\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{y}}^{\mathsf{x}}
of windows can be fused to form another,

\mathnormal{W}_{\mathsf{y}}^{\mathrm{J}}=\bigcup_{\mathsf{x}_{i}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{y}}^{\mathsf{x}}}\mathnormal{W}_{\mathsf{x}_{i}}^{\mathrm{J}}\mathchar 59\relax

(18)

and if their selections are independent,

\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{y}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}})=1-\prod_{\mathsf{x}_{i}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{y}}^{\mathsf{x}}}(1-\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{x}_{i}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}})).

(19)

The number of these subwindows is
\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{y}}^{\mathsf{x}}
. For example, a survey (
{\mathsf{s}}
) is divided into
\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{o}}
observation windows (generically labeled
{\mathsf{o}_{i}}
) from the set
\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathsf{o}}
. The window resulting from fusing two subwindows
{\mathsf{x}}
and
{\mathsf{y}}
in this way can be written
\mathsf{x}\cup\mathsf{y}
. A joint window
\mathsf{x}\cap\mathsf{y}
instead picks an event only if it is selected by both subwindows.

Figure 4:
Illustration of how different selection windows could manifest in a spectrogram. window
{\mathsf{x}}
(shaded blue) can be projected onto time (
\mathsf{x}{:}t
) and frequency (
\mathsf{x}{:}\nu
). A second window
{\mathsf{y}}
(shaded red) can be fused with
{\mathsf{x}}
(violet outline), or a joint selection can be made (gray shading). Also shown are windows picking a specific time (green line), frequency (gold line), or both (black dot).

The last operation on windows is projection, working on a window or host and a fundamental variable
\rho
like time or frequency. The projection
\mathsf{x}{:}\rho
(or
{\mathrm{K}}{:}\rho
) makes a selection according to the range of
\rho
covered by
{\mathsf{x}}
or
{\mathrm{K}}
(Figure
4
). The projection of a window is given by

\mathnormal{W}_{\mathsf{x}{:}\rho}^{\mathrm{J}}=\bigcup_{\rho:\mathnormal{W}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\mathrm{J}}\cap\mathnormal{W}_{\uprho}^{\mathrm{J}}(\rho)\neq\varnothing}\mathnormal{W}_{\uprho}^{\mathrm{J}}(\rho)

(20)

and the projection of an object is

{\mathnormal{W}_{\mathrm{K}{:}\rho}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})=\bigcup_{\rho:\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}}\in\mathnormal{W}_{\uprho}^{\mathrm{K}}(\rho)}}\mathnormal{W}_{\uprho}^{\mathrm{J}}(\rho)

(21)

with
\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{x}{:}\rho}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}};\rho)={\max[}\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\mathrm{J}}({\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}})\mathsf{P}_{\uprho}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}};\rho){|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}}\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}\in\mathnormal{W}_{\uprho}^{\mathrm{J}}(\rho){]}
and
{\mathsf{P}_{\mathrm{K}{:}\rho}^{\mathrm{J}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}};\rho{\mathchar 59\relax\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}}})=\mathbb{I}[\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}\in{\mathnormal{W}_{\mathrm{K}{:}\rho}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})}]
. Projection is a versatile operation, letting us make selections according to a single shared quantity. For example,
\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathsf{x}{:}\nu|\mathrm{b}}
is the effective isotropic energy release of broadcast
{\mathrm{b}}
at
any
time or polarization within the bandwidth of
{\mathsf{x}}
;
\mathnormal{N}_{\mathrm{c}{:}t\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{g}}^{\mathrm{C}}
is the number of communicative societies in galaxy
{\mathrm{g}}
that are ever active during the lifespan of the specific society
{\mathrm{c}}
. Projection is particularly useful when describing broadcasts that can run in and out of a window, like in the chord model later on (Appendix
C
).

By default, this series uses the ALL window (
\infty
) selecting every event in a population when no other window is specified or implied.

III.2.2
Hosts

Specifying a host
{{\mathrm{K}}}
in a selection restricts the population to its subpopulation, as defined by its point process: it draws
\mathrm{J}
-type objects from
{\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})}
. The host-based selection of objects is determined solely by their position on the tree – the object must be the direct ancestor or descendant of the host, or be the host itself. Objects are indexed, and the indices serve to denote their associated host selection as well. Most variables in this series use a host, but when none is otherwise specified, the cosmic host
{\mathrm{U}}
is assumed.

III.2.3
Samples and selection-relative intensities

A full selection
(\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathrm{L}})
, as a combination of a window and a host, selects
\mathrm{J}
objects from the
{\mathsf{x}}
-thinning of the point process
{\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{L}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{L}})}
over the set
\mathnormal{W}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\mathrm{J}}
. The resulting point process,
{\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{L}})}
, is the random sample drawn by
(\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathrm{L}})
. An object
{{\mathrm{J}}}
is selected by
(\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathrm{L}})
(
{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{L}})}
) if and only if:

•

It is within the random sample
\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\mathrm{J}}
; that is, its parameter tuple
{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}}
is within
\mathnormal{W}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\mathrm{J}}
and selected by the
{\mathsf{x}}
-thinning.

•

It is a member of the subpopulation of its host (
{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{L}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{L}})}
), picked by the host selection
{{\mathrm{L}}}
.

•

For each ancestor
{{\mathrm{K}}}
of
{{\mathrm{J}}}
up to and including
{\mathrm{L}}
,
{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}^{\mathrm{K}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{L}})}
; so that each of these ancestors is
also
selected by the window.

This final condition is a somewhat tricky one, but an object can be missed because its ancestor was not sampled. Suppose we defined a window
{\mathsf{x}}
that picked all galaxies and metasocieties in a patch of the sky, but only societies that are older than a million years old, and all their broadcasts. A radio broadcast from a society that is only one thousand years old would not be selected by
(\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathrm{U}})
even though
{\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\mathrm{B}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}})=1}
for all
{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}}
, because its host society is excluded.

If
{\mathrm{L}}
is a host for objects of type
\mathrm{K}\in\aleph(\mathrm{J})
, the intensity relative to a selection
(\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathrm{L}})
is

{\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}^{\mathrm{J}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}{|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{L}}})=\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\mathrm{L}}({\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{L}}})\int_{\mathnormal{W}^{\mathrm{K}}}{\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathrm{L}}^{\mathrm{K}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}}{|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{L}}})\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}}\mathchar 59\relax

(22)

with

\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}})=\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}})\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}})=\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}})\updelta(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}-\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}).

(23)

Integrating gives us the expected number of
\mathrm{J}
-type objects sampled by the selection:

{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}^{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle\equiv}\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{L}})}\right\rangle=\int_{\mathnormal{W}^{\mathrm{J}}}{\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}^{\mathrm{J}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}{|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{L}}})d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}.

(24)

The process of selection often introduces biases in object properties, so
{\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}^{\mathrm{J}}}
is not usually proportional to
{\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathrm{L}}^{\mathrm{J}}}
. Generally, objects that are “larger” are overrepresented in a sample (see section
III.8
).

III.2.4
Simplifying distributions through marginalization, rates, and abundances

The full intensities often involve many parameters that are of no interest in a given problem. The distribution is simplified by marginalizing these irrelevant quantities, treating them as marks to be ignored (Section
II.2
). If a parameter tuple
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}
can be divided into components that we are interested in,
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{J}}
, and those that we are not,
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{J}}
, with
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}=\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{J}}\times\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{J}}
, then

{\mathnormal{\Psi}^{\mathrm{J}\ast}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{J}}{|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}}})=\int{\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}^{\ast}_{\mathrm{J}}\times\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{J}}{|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}}})d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{J}}.

(25)

Of course, this equation applies to
{\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}}
itself, which uses the
\infty
window.

The most common of these marginalized intensities is the rate that objects form, a function over time, and possibly other fundamental variables like frequency. When
\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{J}}
is the time an object is born, the rate is

{\breve{\mathnormal{\Gamma}}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}}(t\mathchar 59\relax\nu{|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}}})=\frac{d\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\upnu\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}}(\nu{\mathchar 59\relax\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}}})\right\rangle}{d\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{J}}}(\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{J}}=t)\mathchar 59\relax

(26)

where
{\mathnormal{N}_{\upnu\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}}(\nu{\mathchar 59\relax\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}}})
is the number of objects hosted by
{\mathrm{K}}
and selected by a window picking all objects that cover the frequency
\nu
. Broadcasts have limited bandwidth, but other types of objects (including societies, metasocieties, and galaxies) are not confined by frequency and have
{\mathnormal{N}_{\upnu\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}}(\nu{\mathchar 59\relax\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}}})={\mathnormal{N}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})}
. If the number of selected objects is proportional to the number of stars (e.g., more stars means more inhabited worlds), a convenient variable is the stellar rate,

{\mathnormal{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}}(t\mathchar 59\relax\nu{|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}}})={\breve{\mathnormal{\Gamma}}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}(t\mathchar 59\relax\nu|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})/\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\star}(t\mathchar 59\relax\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})\right\rangle}\mathchar 59\relax

(27)

where
{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\star}}(t{\mathchar 59\relax\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}}})
is the number of stars extant at time
t
within the host
{\mathrm{K}}
.

If
{\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}}
is marginalized over all parameters, then the resulting zero-dimensional distribution simply gives
\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle
as per Campbell’s formula (equation
4
). This itself can be regarded as a parameter, with
\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle
defined for some well-characterized window
{\mathsf{x}}
scaling any other distribution. For objects that trace stars, the most useful of these scaling parameters is the stellar abundance

{\mathnormal{\Xi}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}}(t\mathchar 59\relax\nu{|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}}})=\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\cap\upnu\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}}(t\mathchar 59\relax\nu{\mathchar 59\relax\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}}})\right\rangle/\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\star}}(t{\mathchar 59\relax\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}}})\right\rangle\mathchar 59\relax

(28)

with
\mathsf{t}\cap\upnu
picking all objects active at both time
t
and, for broadcasts, frequency
\nu
. It is the mean number of objects per star that “cover” a point on a spectrogram.

III.3
Random variables

III.3.1
Singleton variables

A
singleton variable

\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathrm{J}}
is a random variable that describes a single object
{\mathrm{J}}
.
20
20

20

T
Technically, for a random object
{\mathrm{J}}
,
\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}})
is a random function that outputs a random variable from the input
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}
(Section
III.1.3
). The same caveat applies to aggregate “variables” with random hosts.
I assume that it is independent of all other objects, depending only on
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}
. Examples include position of an object, luminosity, flux received at Earth, and total number of photons collected in a telescope from that object in one pixel of a detector. The value of a singleton variable is not always determined by
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}
, only its distribution is. The number of photons counted even from a perfectly coherent laser beacon has detector and shot noise, for example.

The basic notation for singleton variables is
\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}
, where
\mathnormal{\kappa}
may be replaced with another symbol, usually a lowercase letter. In some cases, a random variable depends on how long we are observing, in what frequencies, at what polarizations, as is true for emission measured. The
quantity window

{\mathsf{x}}
sets the bounds of integration for
\mathnormal{\kappa}
(Figure
5
). Generally, an object with no activity or emission within this window is not selected by
{\mathsf{x}}
,

\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}{(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}})}=0~{\Rightarrow}~\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}\notin\mathnormal{W}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\mathrm{J}}{\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}}

(29)

although an object can be excluded for other reasons. When no quantity window is specified, the ALL window implicitly is used (
\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathrm{J}}=\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\infty|\mathrm{J}}
). The basic parameters for each object can be interpreted as special singleton variables that naturally use the ALL window; they are intrinsic properties, unaffected by observation.

A calculation with singleton variables over a population
{\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})}
is performed by marking each point with the value of
\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}{(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}})}
. As noted in Section
II.2
, this is equivalent to considering a point process in
\mathnormal{W}^{\mathrm{J}}\times\mathbb{R}
for a real-valued random variable. As long as the marks are independent of any and all other points in the process, the intensity of this new process is

\frac{d^{2}\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle}{d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}d\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\kappa}{|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}}})={\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}{|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}}}){\cdot}\uppsi[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}](\mathnormal{\kappa}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}).

(30)

III.3.2
Aggregate variables

An
aggregate variable

\mathnormal{K}
is the sum of the singleton variables for a sample of objects drawn from a host
{\mathrm{K}}
:

{\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})}\equiv{\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})}}\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}{(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}})}\mathchar 59\relax

(31)

where
\mathnormal{K}
may be replaced with another symbol, usually an uppercase letter. In addition to the quantity window
{\mathsf{x}}
, a potentially distinct
object window
is combined with the host
{\mathrm{K}}
to define a selection that picks out which of the objects included in a sum. Each
{\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}}
is treated using the marked point process for the corresponding
\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}
.

To simplify notation, the quantity window may be omitted to indicate that it is the same as the object window:
\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}\equiv\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}
(Table
5
). Applying equation
29
allows us to “expand” or “contract” the object window:

{{\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}=\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}}~\text{if}~(\forall\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}\in\mathnormal{W}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\mathrm{J}})~\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}})=\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{y}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}})}.

(32)

Aggregate and singleton variables can be interconverted,

{\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{y}|\mathrm{K}}\leftrightarrow\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}}\mathchar 59\relax

(33)

where the object window of a singleton variable is always identical to its quantity window. These simply reflect different ways of viewing the quantity: the aggregate variable interprets it as a sum over a population, while the singleton variable interprets it as an intrinsic property of the host. A common example in extragalactic astronomy is the mass of a galaxy’s stars – whether it is viewed as the collective property of a stellar population or a property of a single galaxy, it is still the same quantity. This lets us build high-level aggregates recursively, like the total emission from all broadcasts in all societies in a galaxy.

Number variables can be interpreted as a special case of aggregate variables:

{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})}={\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})}}1=|{\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})}|.

(34)

III.4
Selection-relative probabilities, means, and variances: The effects of selection bias

A distribution operation
{\mathbb{O}}
takes a variable and returns a function or number derived from its probability distribution – it needs a random variable, and cannot stand on its own. These operations include the CDF, PDF, mean, and variance. In this section, I consider how these operations are affected by selection biases.

III.4.1
Selection-relative probabilities

Let us say we want to know the probability of an event
\mathnormal{E}_{\mathrm{J}}
describing some property of
\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}
. Now, this probability is associated with a random object
{\mathrm{J}}
and thus is actually a random function of
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}
. Our general assumption has been that the probability distribution of any random variable
\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}
is fully determined by
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}
, automatically implying independence between other such variables and the number of objects. This is the simple probability of the event,
\mathnormal{P}(\mathnormal{E}_{\mathrm{J}})\equiv\mathnormal{P}(\mathnormal{E}_{\mathrm{J}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}})
.

In turn, the probability
\mathnormal{P}(\mathnormal{E}_{\mathrm{J}})
is also a function of
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}
. However, any distribution for the
\mathrm{J}
objects themselves allows us to generate a mixture distribution for
\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}
, and then we can evaluate the probability of
\mathnormal{E}_{\mathrm{J}}
for this mixture. This includes distributions modified by selection windows. When the value of a random variable
\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}
for a single object is independent of all other objects, the selection-relative probability is thus defined

{\mathnormal{P}_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}}(\mathnormal{E}_{\mathrm{J}})\equiv\frac{1}{\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle}\int_{\mathnormal{W}_{\mathsf{y}}^{\mathrm{J}}}{\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})}\mathnormal{P}(\mathnormal{E}_{\mathrm{J}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}})d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}.

(35)

This is the probability that
\mathnormal{E}_{\mathrm{J}}
occurs for a
\mathrm{J}
-type object fairly drawn from the random sample
\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})
. It is an ensemble probability, averaging over all possible selections, not just for a realized sample. It reflects the bias of a selection.

An archetypal event is
\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}
being within some specified range. The previous equation immediately yields a selection-relative CDF, with
{F\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}(x)\equiv\mathnormal{P}_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}(\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\leq x)}
. Differentiating then gives the selection-relative PDF,

{\uppsi\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}}({\mathnormal{\kappa}})\equiv\\
\frac{1}{\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle}\int_{\mathnormal{W}_{\mathsf{y}}^{\mathrm{J}}}{\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})}\uppsi[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}]({\mathnormal{\kappa}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}})d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}\mathchar 59\relax

(36)

where
\uppsi[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}]
is the (unbiased) simple probability distribution for
\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}
determined by
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}
. An analogous equation can be written for aggregate variables.

Note that the objects sampled by a selection are random. Thus, selection-relative probabilities and operations are used on things describing random objects, although the selection itself may have a realized host.
21
21

21

I
If for some reason a selection-relative operation were applied to a variable describing a realized object, the selection would drop out, and it would become a simple operation described in the next subsection (e.g.,
\uppsi\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{j}}\right]_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}=\uppsi[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{j}}]
).

III.4.2
A notational convention for simple and selection-relative operations

The simple probabilities are selection-relative probabilities where the self-point process is being used. This rule extends to all distribution operations on the probability:

\displaystyle\mathbb{O}[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{O}[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}]=\mathbb{O}\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}

\displaystyle\mathbb{O}[\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{O}[\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}}]=\mathbb{O}\left[\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}

(37)

including mean, variance, and order statistics.

A similar simplification is used in this series for windows – a selection-relative distribution operation “missing” its window inherits it from the variable’s object window (which is the quantity window too for singleton variables):

{\mathbb{O}\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathrm{L}}}\equiv{\mathbb{O}\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}}~\text{and}~{\mathbb{O}\left[\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathrm{L}}}\equiv{\mathbb{O}\left[\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}}\mathchar 59\relax

(38)

including mean and variance (Table
5
). Contrast with the convention for variables, which stand on their own and have no other variable to draw a window from. This convention is motivated by practicality: in most windows we use, the vast majority of objects do not contribute to aggregate quantities (equation
29
) and do not concern us. When we want to know the mean broadcast fluence over an observation, we do not want to include those that were only visible during the Jurassic, for instance.
22
22

22

If we
do
want to include these objects, we apply the ALL window operator,
{\mathbb{O}\left[X\right]_{\infty\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}}
(compare Figure
5
).
Remember, if the variable itself does not specify a window, it uses the ALL (
\infty
) window (Section
III.2.1
), which the mean then inherits (
{\mathbb{O}\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathrm{K}}}={\mathbb{O}\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\infty|\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\infty\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}}
). This applies to the intrinsic quantities describing objects like their lifespan and position.

III.4.3
Simple means for a single object

The most straightforward averaging operation is the simple mean. Say we have a singleton quantity
\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}
associated with a random object of type
\mathrm{J}
with a parameter tuple
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}
. Its simple mean is:

\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle\equiv\left\langle{\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}})}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle=\int{\mathnormal{\kappa}}\cdot\uppsi[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}](\mathnormal{\kappa}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}})d\mathnormal{\kappa}.

(39)

This is a function of
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}
. The simple mean of an aggregate variable is, with the help of equation
33
,

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv\left\langle\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})\middle|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\int{\mathnormal{K}}\cdot\uppsi[\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}](\mathnormal{K}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})d\mathnormal{K}.

(40)

Recall that
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}}
does
not
determine the subpopulation of
{\mathrm{K}}
’s descendants, only its statistical properties. The mean is therefore an average over all realizations of this subpopulation, conditionalized on the properties of the host. This sampling variance within the host is regarded as “noise.”

The average is found by applying Campbell’s formula (equation
4
) to the intensity of the marked point process:

\left\langle{\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle=\int_{\mathnormal{W}_{\mathsf{y}}^{\mathrm{J}}}\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle{\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})}d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}.

(41)

The integral for
\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle
in equation
39
is just a special case of this, summing over
\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{J}}{(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}})}
.

III.4.4
Selection-relative means

But what if we want to average, not just over the “noise” in a random variable, but over different possible “draws” of its associated object? This is what a selection-relative mean does (Figure
5
). For a selection
(\mathsf{z}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathrm{L}})
, the selection-relative mean is

\displaystyle{\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{z}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}}

\displaystyle\equiv\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}{(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}})}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}\in{\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{z}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{L}})}\right\rangle

\displaystyle{\left\langle\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{z}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}}

\displaystyle\equiv\left\langle\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}{(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})}\middle|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}}\in{\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{z}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}^{\mathrm{K}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{L}})}\right\rangle.

(42)

Means with respect to realized hosts have analogous definitions. Unlike the simple means, the selection-relative means averages over all the different possible parameter tuples for the object being described by the random variable, weighted by the probability of it being a part of the sample drawn by the selection. It is an
ensemble
average, the mean that we would get if we repeated the selection again and again.

To give a concrete example, suppose we had a society
{\mathrm{c}}
in a galaxy
{\mathrm{g}}
, and we observe it over a window
{\mathsf{o}}
. The total energy received from all broadcasts in that society over
{\mathsf{o}}
is
\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{B}}
. Then
\left\langle\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{c}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle
is the mean value of the energy we receive from the broadcasts in that society, or more precisely, the average for an ensemble of societies each with an identical parameter tuple
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{c}}
. However,
\left\langle\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{g}}
is the mean value over all the possible societies in the galaxy, roughly the value of
\mathfrak{E}_{\mathsf{o}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}
expected from a typical society.

Figure 5:
Comparison of different quantity windows and selections applied to a variable and an averaging operation. Here we have a cluster of
\mathrm{J}
-type spherical objects in a host
{\mathrm{k}}
, observed in the sky field as pictured at upper right. A survey
{\mathsf{s}}
covers this field with two observations,
{\mathsf{o}_{\mathsf{A}}}
and
{\mathsf{o}_{\mathsf{B}}}
, each an aperture. Any object that touches a window is selected by it. The subplots depict different multiwindow selection-relative means, with simplified (above) and full (below) notation. The underlying variable
\mathnormal{\kappa}
is a quantity that is integrated over its quantity window (e.g., starlight from a galaxy that falls within the window), with the amount falling within its bounds indicated by the shading. The means average this quantity over only some of the objects, the ones outlined by rings. The multiwindow means on the bottom average over several observations;
{\mathsf{o}_{i}}
is a placeholder for
{\mathsf{o}_{\mathsf{A}}}
and
{\mathsf{o}_{\mathsf{B}}}
. Note however that the means are actually ensemble averages, over all possible configurations of the objects, whereas only one realized configuration is shown. Other operations like the variance and PDF also have multiwindow selection-relative versions analogous to the means shown here.

The selection-relative mean is found using the selection-relative (biased) PDF (equation
36
). Assuming all the variables are mutually independent, equation
39
gives us

{\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{z}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}}=\frac{1}{\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{z}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle}\int_{\mathnormal{W}_{\mathsf{z}}^{\mathrm{J}}}\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle{\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathsf{z}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{L}})}d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}\mathchar 59\relax

(43)

with the selection-relative mean of an aggregate variable found with an analogous integral (equation
33
). A consequence of Campbell’s formula is that

\left\langle{\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle=\left\langle{\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})}}\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}{(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}})}\right\rangle=\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle{\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}}.

(44)

Once completed, the averages may themselves be averaged, most naturally when the inner average’s selection host is a random object.
23
23

23

I
If the operation’s host is a realized object
{\mathrm{k}}
, then the selection-relative mean (or variance) has a fixed value determined by
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{k}}
(i.e., it is a degenerate random variable); whereas, if the operation’s host is random object
{\mathrm{K}}
, then it is a function that yields a single value for each
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}}
, or equivalently, the degenerate random variable that always attains that value.
As a random variable, this variable inherits the object window and host from the averaging operator itself:
\left\langle X_{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}\rightarrow{\mathnormal{\kappa}^{\prime}_{\mathsf{y}|\mathrm{K}}}{(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})}
.

Note that, generally,

{\left\langle\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{z}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}\neq\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{z}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}}\mathchar 59\relax

(45)

and similarly for aggregate variables. That is because the left-hand side averages over subselections before averaging over the outermost selection. Some subselections may have more
\mathrm{J}
-type objects than others, but these are all weighted equally on the left-handed side, despite their different population sizes. By evenly sampling the
\mathrm{K}
-type host objects, the left-hand side fails to evenly sample the
\mathrm{J}
-type objects like the right-hand side. This effect is related to Simpson’s Paradox
[
75
]
. However, it
is
true that

{\left\langle\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle\right\rangle_{\mathsf{z}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}=\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{z}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}}\mathchar 59\relax

(46)

because the inner mean’s selection necessarily has a population size of
1
– this is just the law of total expectation and an expression of Campbell’s formula for marked point processes.

III.4.5
Simple and selection-relative variances

The simple variance of a variable is found using the distribution of that variable that comes from its tuple. Selection-relative variances then average over a distribution of random objects. Their definition follows from that of a general conditional variance,
\mathbb{V}\left[X|Y\right]=\left\langle X^{2}|Y\right\rangle-\left\langle X|Y\right\rangle^{2}

[
178
,
15
]
. Thus
{\mathbb{V}\left[X\right]_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}=\left\langle X^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}-\left\langle X\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{2}}
.

The law of total variance is an important tool in evaluating both types of variances. By first conditionalizing on
\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})
, we derive the simple variance of
\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})
:

{\mathbb{V}\left[\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}\right]=\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{2}\mathbb{V}\left[\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}\right]+\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle\mathbb{V}\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}}

(47)

as long as all of the variables are independent. When
{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})}
for a fixed
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}}
is also Poisson, the variance reduces to

{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}=\int_{\mathnormal{W}^{\mathrm{J}}}\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}^{2}\right\rangle\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

equivalent to Campbell’s second formula (equation
10
). The law also gives us, for selection-relative variances,

{\mathbb{V}\left[{\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right]_{\mathsf{z}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}=\mathbb{V}\left[\left\langle{\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle\right]_{\mathsf{z}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}+\left\langle\mathbb{V}\left[{\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right]\right\rangle_{\mathsf{z}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}.}

(48)

III.5
Multiwindow operations

An observing program is usually a collection of many observations: a survey
{\mathsf{s}}
might take several pointings
{\uppi_{i}}
, each in turn involving thousands of distinct observations
{\mathsf{o}_{j}}
. The problem is that the statistical properties of a sample may depend on which subwindow we are considering. Differences between subwindows also introduce still more variance. When mapping a spiral galaxy, for instance, the stars are far more dense in the core than the outer disk, increasing the variance in star counts because the underlying density changes. We need to be able to ask questions like “What is the expected number of stars per observation averaged over all observations in the survey?” and “What’s the maximum broadcast fluence during an observation among all the observations we do?”

Just as the selection-relative operations have an object window that picks which objects are included in the operation, the multiwindow operations have a quantity window, which defines which subwindows are considered. The most general operation is written
\mathbb{O}\left[X\right]_{\mathsf{z}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}
, where
{\mathsf{z}}
is its object window, and
(\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K})
is its selection.

For our purposes, there are a fixed number of subwindows with specified properties. I define the PDF as a mixture of the PDFs for the individual windows:

\uppsi\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{z}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}(\mathnormal{\kappa}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})\equiv\frac{1}{\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{z}}^{\mathsf{x}}}\sum_{i=1}^{\mathsf{N}_{\mathsf{z}}^{\mathsf{x}}}\uppsi\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}(\mathnormal{\kappa}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}}).

(49)

The general multiwindow mean is

\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{z}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}\equiv\left\langle\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}_{i}|\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}|\mathsf{x}_{i}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{z}}^{\mathsf{x}}\right\rangle.

(50)

We simply take the mean over the given selection for each object window, then take the average over the object windows (Figure
5
). The general multiwindow variance is, by the law of total variance,

\mathbb{V}\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{z}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}\equiv\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{z}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}-\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{z}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{2}\\
=\left\langle\mathbb{V}\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}_{i}|\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}|\mathsf{x}_{i}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{z}}^{\mathsf{x}}\right\rangle+\mathbb{V}\left[\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}_{i}|\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}|\mathsf{x}_{i}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{z}}^{\mathsf{x}}\right].

(51)

The second term in the final equality is the variance due to the changes in the population between
{\mathsf{x}_{i}}
windows. Finally the general multiwindow maximum is

\mathrm{max}\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{z}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}\equiv\mathrm{max}[\mathrm{max}\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}_{i}|\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}|\mathsf{x}_{i}\in\mathsf{S}_{\mathsf{z}}^{\mathsf{x}}]\mathchar 59\relax

(52)

and likewise for the minimum. Thus, the mean energy fluence from a broadcast during a typical observation in a survey
{\mathsf{s}}
among the broadcasts the survey selects from a galaxy
{\mathrm{g}}
is written
\left\langle\mathfrak{h}_{\mathsf{o}_{i}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{s}|\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{g}}
, for instance.

The populations in different windows are not generally independent. Adjacent windows can cover the same objects: the sidelobes of one pointing can intersect the primary beam of another for instance. Sometimes the objects themselves are extended, crossing into different windows, as when a frequency-drifting narrowband broadcast cuts across many channels. The greatest effect of this cross-correlation is on the maxima and minima because they reduce the effective number of independent samples. If the subwindows are very broad, the variance can be reduced as well, because the same objects are covered in all pointings, averaging out each measurement.

When working with multiwindow operations, there is a distinction between general unspecified windows and specific windows, similar to that between random objects and realized objects. We can assign a unique label to a unique window, like
{\mathsf{o}_{1}}
or
{\mathsf{o}_{2}}
, and apply it to a variable. This fixes a unique subwindow; the operation’s object window becomes irrelevant because the subwindow is one of a kind. In other cases, we really do want to consider the full gamut of subwindows in a collection, and to emphasize this, we might use a subwindow label like
{\mathsf{o}_{i}}
, where
i
is a free variable. Most often, I assume that the statistical properties of the population do not change appreciably from window to window. Then the multiwindow selection-relative means and variances simply collapse to the selection-relative means and variances, and the multiwindow maxima are a simple application of extreme value theory (Appendix
A
). Only then can we elide the distinction between unspecified and specified windows: any observation is just an observation
{\mathsf{o}}
.

The multiwindow operations come with their own notational simplifications. The quantity window can be inherited from the operation’s selection but not the variable, while an operation labeled
solely
by a window uses it as a quantity window:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{O}\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}_{i}|\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}}

\displaystyle\equiv{\mathbb{O}\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}_{i}|\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{y}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}}

\displaystyle{\mathbb{O}\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}_{i}|\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{y}}}

\displaystyle\equiv{\mathbb{O}\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}_{i}|\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{y}|\mathsf{x}_{i}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}}

(53)

Table
5
summarizes all of the notation rules, and Figure
5
provides an illustrated example. As before, the object window of an operation is inherited from the variable, even when a separate quantity window is specified:

\mathbb{O}\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}_{i}|\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{y}|\mathrm{K}}\equiv\mathbb{O}\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}_{i}|\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{y}|\mathsf{x}_{i}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}.

(54)

\mathbb{O}[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}]

\mathbb{O}\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{x}|\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}

\mathbb{O}[\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}]

\mathbb{O}\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathrm{K}}

\mathbb{O}\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{x}|\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}

\mathbb{O}\left[\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathrm{L}}

\mathbb{O}\left[\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{y}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}

\mathbb{O}\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{y}}

\mathbb{O}\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{y}|\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}

\mathbb{O}\left[\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{z}}

\mathbb{O}\left[\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{z}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}

\mathbb{O}\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}

\mathbb{O}\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{y}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}

\mathbb{O}\left[\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{z}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}

\mathbb{O}\left[\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{z}|\mathsf{z}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}

\mathbb{O}\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{y}|\mathrm{K}}

\mathbb{O}\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{y}|\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}

\mathbb{O}\left[\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{z}|\mathrm{L}}

\mathbb{O}\left[\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{z}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}

Note. — The simplifications are applied from the inside out. Thus,
\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}\to\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\infty|\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}\to\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\infty|\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{y}|\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}
. When the host of a selection-relative operation (including a general multiwindow operation) is a random object, the result should be understood as a function of the host’s properties.

III.6
Regularization of variables with broad distributions

Like other astrophysical entities, ETIs and technosignatures may be described by quantities with heavy-tailed distributions. The radiated energy is widely thought to be a possible example
[
48
]
; others may include the lifespan of societies or transmitters, or the number of societies per metasociety. Plausible distributions like shallow power laws can have variances and means dominated by very rare events with extreme properties. These objects may be too uncommon to detect in any practical selection, however, and thus throw off estimates for the properties of a typical sample.

When needed, this series estimates mean and variance by trimming values of a random variable that are far outside the usual range in a typical sample. This regularization is done by imposing cutoff values
\mathnormal{\kappa}^{L}
and
\mathnormal{\kappa}^{H}
based on extreme value statistics (Appendix
A
). Starting from a random variable
\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}
and trimming with respect to a selection
(\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathrm{K}})
, the regularized variable
{{\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}^{[\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}]}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}};\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})}
has a truncated distribution,

\uppsi[{{\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}^{[\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}]}}}](\mathnormal{\kappa})\equiv\displaystyle\frac{\uppsi[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}](\mathnormal{\kappa})\cdot\mathbb{I}[\mathnormal{\kappa}^{L}\leq\mathnormal{\kappa}\leq\mathnormal{\kappa}^{H}]}{{\mathnormal{P}_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}}(\mathnormal{\kappa}^{L}\leq\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\leq\mathnormal{\kappa}^{H})}.

(55)

Equation
55
also regularizes aggregate variables, as they can be treated as just a special case of singleton variables (equation
33
). Because aggregate variables are sums, the calculations are more complicated. If the individual contributions have a narrow enough distribution that their full range is expected to be well-sampled in each aggregate, any large fluctuations in the sum must be due to fluctuations in the number of objects included:
\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J};[\mathsf{y}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathrm{L}]}\approx\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J};[\mathsf{y}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathrm{L}]}\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}
. These fluctuations may be relatively small and the regularization has essentially no effect. If the individual contributions have a very broad distribution – when we are calculating the aggregate luminosity of objects with a shallow power-law distribution, for example – the sum is almost entirely dominated by the largest contribution in the sample and
\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J};[\mathsf{y}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}\mathrm{L}]}\approx\mathrm{max}\left[\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{y}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}
.

The cutoff is a statistical quantity, however, and any actual sample may include extreme outliers. Thus this regularization will underestimate the sample mean and variance some of the time, possibly by a large degree in rare instances.

III.7
The discreteness criterion

In order to make a detection of a broadcast in a survey
{\mathsf{s}}
, at least one must be sampled during a program
24
24

24

The converse is not always true – a sampled broadcast may be too faint to detect, or confused with many others.
, and a null result should occur whenever zero broadcasts are sampled. It is possible our observation was too short, too narrowband, or had too small a field to intercept rare broadcasts even when they are extremely bright. This happens outside of SETI too: a supernova or a quasar episode can boost a galaxy’s luminosity far above its typical value for a short time. If a galaxy’s measured luminosity is fainter than a supernova or a quasar, that just means one was not active during the observation, not that they never are found there. Thus, a null result is compatible with any models with a high enough probability of zero broadcasts being detected.

The discreteness criterion states that we should only expect a detection in survey
{\mathsf{s}}
with models having

\mathnormal{P}(\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathrm{B}}=0)\leq{\bar{\mathnormal{P}}}

(56)

for a suitably conservative false negative threshold
\bar{\mathnormal{P}}
near
0
. Only models that fulfill this criterion are likely to be constrained by observations. To order of magnitude, we expect a nonempty sample only if the mean number of objects is
\gtrsim 1
.
25
25

25

This follows from Markov’s inequality, which gives
\mathnormal{P}({\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathrm{J}}}\geq 1)\leq\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle
for the nonnegative
{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathrm{J}}}

[
178
]
.
However, no broadcasts will be detected if the sample is missing any of the ancestor nodes on the “tree” – societies, metasocieties, or galaxies. Equation
56
implies the often-simpler formula

{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{s}}^{\mathrm{K}}\right\rangle}\geq 1-{\bar{\mathnormal{P}}}\approx 1~{\text{if}~\mathrm{K}\in\aleph(\mathrm{B})~\text{or}~\mathrm{K}=\mathrm{B}.}

(57)

III.8
An example: lifespan bias in time-limited selection

Long-lived objects are disproportionately represented in a field once a population has reached equilibrium. This phenomenon serves as an example of how to make selection-relative calculations.

Suppose we have objects of type
\mathrm{J}
in a host
{\mathrm{K}}
. How is the probability distribution of object lifespan affected by a time-limited selection? To start, let us suppose that each such object forms at time
\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{J}}
and exists for duration
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}}
without interruption. The distribution function for these objects has the form

{\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})}=\frac{d^{3}\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle}{d\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}}d\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{J}}d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{J}}}{(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})}

(58)

where
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{J}}
stands for a tuple of other, irrelevant parameters to marginalize over. Now consider a window
{\mathsf{x}}
that selects all objects in
{\mathrm{K}}
active anytime during the contiguous time interval
[\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{x}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{x}}+\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}]
. Clearly, an object in this population is selected if it either formed before
\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{x}}
and lives long enough to overlap the window (
\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{x}}-\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}}\leq\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{J}}<\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{x}}
) or if it forms within the window (
\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{x}}\leq\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{J}}\leq\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{x}}+\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}})
. Thus, if the objects form at a constant rate
{\breve{\mathnormal{\Gamma}}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}}
(equation
27
),

\displaystyle\frac{d\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}}\right\rangle}{d\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}}}{(\mathnormal{\tau}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{x}}-{\mathnormal{\tau}}}^{\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{x}}+\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}}\int{\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})}d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{J}}d\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{J}}

\displaystyle=({\mathnormal{\tau}}+\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}){\cdot\breve{\mathnormal{\Gamma}}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})\cdot}{\uppsi\left[\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathrm{K}}(\mathnormal{\tau})}

(59)

from equation
24
, where
{\uppsi\left[\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathrm{K}}}
is the unbiased lifespan distribution. It follows immediately that
{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle=(\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{K}}+\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}})\cdot\breve{\mathnormal{\Gamma}}_{\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})}
, giving us the
{\mathsf{x}}
-biased duration distribution (equation
36
)

{\uppsi\left[\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}(\mathnormal{\tau})}=\frac{{\mathnormal{\tau}}+\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}}{{\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{K}}}+\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}}{\uppsi\left[\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathrm{K}}(\mathnormal{\tau})}.

(60)

Finally, we have (equation
43
),

{\left\langle f(\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}})\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}}=\frac{{\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}}f(\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{K}}}+\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}{\left\langle f(\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{K}}}}{{\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{K}}}+\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}}.

(61)

These formulae assume that the rate has been constant infinitely far into the past, at least in that each object’s lifespan is shorter than the age of
{\mathrm{K}}
. Otherwise, an additional cutoff is needed for the integration in equation
59
, as is well known in the field of stellar populations
[
130
, e.g.,]
. In the limit that all the objects are more short-lived than the host’s age at the start of
{\mathsf{x}}
,
\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{x}}-\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{K}}
, equation
61
still applies. On the other hand, if most all the
\mathrm{J}
objects live longer than
\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{x}}-\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{K}}
, it can be shown the bias disappears: almost every object that has ever been born is still around, so we get a fair sample of them.

Figure 6:
Lifespan bias by a window
{\mathsf{x}}
in a population of objects with exponentially distributed lifespans. Selected objects are in blue (bright blue within the window). On the left, the objects are scattered in spacetime, and their lifespans are intervals in time. Those with longer
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}}
are more likely to reach the window, which covers a fixed span of real time (blue shading). On the right, the objects are represented by points in a simplified haystack (spatial position not included). The window covers a bigger
\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{J}}
range as
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}}
increases.

When the observational window is short compared to typical lifespans, equation
61
effectively weights the biased average by another power of
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}}
. Every biased moment depends on the next higher unbiased moment, save the zeroth where a cancellation occurs. For the mean lifespan in particular,
{\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}}={\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{K}}}+{\mathbb{V}\left[\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathrm{K}}}/({\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{K}}}+\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}})\geq{\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{K}}}
. Hence, one can replicate the result in
Kipping et al. [103]
that
{\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}}\approx 2{\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{K}}}
for exponentially distributed
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}}
. Or, one can show that the lifespan’s selection-relative mean is ill-behaved for a power-law distribution
{\uppsi\left[\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}}\right]_{\mathrm{K}}}\propto\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}}^{-\alpha}
extending to infinity unless
\alpha>3
.

Lifespan bias can be thought of in two ways (Figure
6
). In real spacetime, “bigger” objects are more likely to cross into a window with fixed duration, having a larger “cross-section.” In the haystack, the window itself becomes wider for longer lifespans and thus has a longer reach for these objects. A similar bias applies to band-limited selection by frequency: wideband broadcasts are more likely to be intercepted, a significant fact in the chord model presented later.

Figure
6
helps illustrate the different types of means. Each dot represents a specific, realized object;
\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{j}}\right\rangle
is the simple mean of the lifespan for that object
{\mathrm{j}}
, which is entirely determined by the dot’s position in the haystack. Then
\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle=\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}{(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}})}
is a
function
ranging over the entire space that returns the average lifespan for an object given its parameters – it returns the
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}}
coordinate in the right-hand panel. A realized host
{\mathrm{k}}
has an accompanying distribution of objects over the haystack:
\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{k}}
averages over the entire haystack, while
\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{k}}
averages only over the blue-shaded region. One such realization of the distribution is shown in the figure. Likewise, for a random host
{\mathrm{K}}
, the distribution itself is a function of
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}}
, as are
\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{K}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})
and
\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{K}})
.

IV
Assumptions about randomness

Technosignatures are the result of complex biological, social, and technological phenomena, with a panoply of factors shaping their populations. The full interplay responsible for broadcast characteristics may be analytically intractable. Nonetheless, treating broadcasts as the result of a hierarchy of conditionally independent point processes illuminates our understanding of the ETI populations.

IV.1
“Random” ETIs

Random variables are a very general concept (Section
II.1
) and can describe deterministic events. Still, the rest of the paper views broadcasts and other objects as unpredictable. This does not mean that the intelligences behind them have no specific motivations. Random processes can be used to describe human behavior by focusing on statistical trends rather than the unpredictable specific outcomes resulting from complex motivations
[
96
, e.g.,]
; the same seems plausible for ETIs.

The most basic properties of metasocieties and societies are where they are, when they start, and how long they last. The first two of these likely involve stochastic elements. Potential habitats are scattered randomly through the galaxy, and the timing of ETI evolution likely involves some contingency, although subsequent interstellar expansion might not.

Broadcasts can have predictable structure, though. Predictability can be a byproduct of how a transmitter operates. A narrowbeam transmitter on a rotating world sweeps past the Earth at regular intervals, for example
[
69
]
. ETIs could even exploit periodicity to encode information or make the artificial nature of their broadcasts obvious
[
18
,
80
, as in]
.

However, although broadcasts may have predictable features, their properties are not fully determined – otherwise, we would simply aim our telescopes where and when we expect a broadcast instead of sifting through the haystack. Broadcasts plausibly turn on and off at unpredictable times, the result of unknowable social factors. Randomness in the host galaxy also induces randomness in broadcasts. Each host star has random light travel delay times, parallaxes, and Doppler shifts, and sits in a turbulent interstellar medium with random dispersion and scintillation. A faint periodic broadcast might only be observable during rare moments when scintillation magnifies it to a detectable level
[
39
]
.

The real issue is whether the treatment of broadcasts as independent isolated bursts of radiation is inadequate, and thus the haystack is defined according to the wrong parameters. Introducing dependence is one way to address this: in a periodic train, knowing two subsequent arrivals allows us to predict the others. Often there is a natural parameterization in which we can treat the entire complex of signals as a single broadcast, however, like defining a periodic train by its period and phase. Although the specific distributions used in this paper would not apply, the general ideas of the analysis still do.

IV.2
Independence

I also assume the properties of two sibling objects are independent of each other, conditionalized on the shared parent’s parameter tuple. Likewise, the number of children objects in a parent is conditionally independent of each child object’s parameters. Thus, each broadcast is assumed to be independent of every other, except in the sense they depend on a shared ancestor (society, metasociety, galaxy, or the universe); societies are independent of each other, aside from
their
shared ancestors; and so on.

Independence is by no means obvious. On the broadcast level, although beacons and noncommunicative broadcasts are plausibly completely independent, intragalactic communications are part of a web of messages, providing a natural mechanism for coordination. Broadcasts may be more common if there are more potential receiving societies, and some may even be direct replies to others. Metasocieties can converge to a common broadcast protocol, like designated frequency channels. Dependence could extend to other object types – if interstellar expansion is rampant, the existence of a society around one star strongly suggests the existence of them around nearby stars, and so on.

The framework handles all such seeming dependence by moving the shared properties up the tree. Instead of viewing sibling objects as mutually dependent, we instead postulate that the “dependent quantities” are actually properties of the ancestor itself. Thus, the common properties all follow from the ancestral tuple, which governs the populations within it.
Conditionalized
on that tuple, the siblings are independent of each other. The difference is that the population does not respond to its own fluctuations.

Consider: what if each galaxy has exactly one society arising at a random time since the Big Bang and the society’s lifespan is always one hundred years? Without knowing when the society existed, a single broadcast from that society could occur any time in cosmic history. Once we know when one broadcast occurred, however, any others had to have been made to within one hundred years because there was no broadcasting society at other times – a failure of independence. However, that is only because the broadcast tells us when the society appeared. If we already knew that, learning when one broadcast happens does not necessarily give us any new information – the broadcast times can be independent conditional on societal parameters.

The model can be altered to include different levels to allow for still further degrees of dependence, but it nonetheless assumes that everything can be grouped into discrete objects sorted into hierarchical levels. Perhaps ETI distributions are more like scale-free fractals with a continuous range of dependence. This would necessitate a different kind of framework.

Just because the properties of one object are conditionally independent of another’s does not mean that the parameters of a single object are mutually independent. Massive stars are younger on average; metasocieties encompassing more worlds could live longer; “gregarious” societies could transmit broadcasts at a higher rate and make them brighter on average; and so on. This just means that the joint distribution is not separable into the product of the PDFs of the variables; like when all the objects fall along a line or surface in the haystack. It prevents us from modeling the aggregate emission with compound Poisson statistics. The distribution can still be calculated, however, and we still find the mean with Campbell’s formula (equation
4
). If the societal population is Poissonian, equation
10
allows us to find the variance.
