\mathnormal{\Xi}^{\mathrm{C}}

\mathnormal{\Xi}^{\mathrm{B}}

\cdots

Total temporal (formation) rate

\breve{\mathnormal{\Gamma}}^{\mathrm{J}}

\cdots

\cdots

\breve{\mathnormal{\Gamma}}^{\mathrm{M}}

\breve{\mathnormal{\Gamma}}^{\mathrm{C}}

\breve{\mathnormal{\Gamma}}^{\mathrm{B}}

\breve{\mathnormal{\Gamma}}^{\star}

Arbitrary aggregate variable

\mathnormal{K}^{\mathrm{J}}

\cdots

\mathnormal{K}^{\mathrm{G}}

\mathnormal{K}^{\mathrm{M}}

\mathnormal{K}^{\mathrm{C}}

\mathnormal{K}^{\mathrm{B}}

\mathnormal{K}^{\star}

Note. — The
\mathrm{J}
type objects are generic, used for general formulae, as are the
\mathrm{K}
and
\mathrm{L}
types.

Singleton variables (upper half) are here marked with the uppercase index for a type of object (e.g.,
\mathrm{B}
for broadcasts), if the variable is treated as a general parameter instead of describing a fixed object. A lowercase index (defaults given for each type) in a singleton variable indicates the variable refers to a realized object with fixed parameters. Other indices may be substituted, however, if they are defined for realized objects. Singleton variables may also specify a quantity window.

Aggregate variables (lower half) can also be marked with an object selection, and a quantity window if different from object window. By default, the windows are the ALL (
\infty
) window and the universe (
{\mathrm{U}}
) as a host.

V.1
The universe

The universe is the root node of the tree (Figure
1
), the “object” that contains all the other objects as descendants. Even the universe has a parameter tuple,
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{U}}
, and a haystack, the space of all such tuples
\mathnormal{W}^{\mathrm{U}}
(Table
6
lists relevant notation.). Cosmological parameters like the Hubble constant can be used to define this haystack, but in this work, I assume these are set to fixed values. Instead, the model parameters describe the descendant populations of ETIs and their broadcasts. For instance, suppose we thought that in every transmitter population, the luminosity distribution follows a power-law distribution, all with the same slope
\mathnormal{\gamma}
. We do not know what the slope is, however. Then we can consider
\mathnormal{\gamma}_{\mathrm{U}}
as a cosmic-level parameter, one describing all ETI populations in the Universe. In a given model universe
{\mathrm{u}}
, it takes on a value
\mathnormal{\gamma}_{\mathrm{u}}
, which all the lower-level populations inherit, and then we make calculations for each
\mathnormal{\gamma}_{\mathrm{u}}
variate to compare predictions.

As discussed in Section
III.1.2
, no universe distribution is considered here, only the consequences of different
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{U}}
for observed populations.

V.2
Galaxies as domains for ETIs

ETIs can coordinate their properties on large scale through replication and communication, but there must be a limit to how far these processes can operate. We can imagine the Universe ends up broken up into a patchwork of regions over which coordination succeeded, much like the magnetic domains in a piece of iron
[
141
, see]
. In this series, a domain is an astrophysical region so large and isolated that ETIs cannot spread outside of it, completely confining any metasocieties. Depending on how difficult space travel is, a domain might be as small as a planetary body or as large as the entire Hubble volume. Every domain has sites where individual societies can reside. This work treats galaxies as the natural scale for domains.
26
26

26

I
If intergalactic travel or communication is allowed, galactic metasocieties could fuse into an intergalactic metasociety on the domain scale. If cohesive galactic submetasocieties persist, then these would be the children of the top-level intergalactic metasocieties, instead of being the parents. We could also consider galaxies analogously to stars in Section
V.3
.

Although they are home to entire populations of discrete stars and worlds, galaxies themselves form a cosmic population with a distribution

\displaystyle{\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathrm{U}}^{\mathrm{G}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{G}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{U}})}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{d\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathrm{U}}^{\mathrm{G}}}\right\rangle}{d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{G}}}{(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{G}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{U}})}

\displaystyle=\frac{d^{3}\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathrm{U}}^{\mathrm{G}}\right\rangle}{d\mathnormal{N}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\star}d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\mathrm{G}}d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{G}}}{(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{G}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{U}})}.

(62)

The two parameters I single out are the number of stars in the entire galaxy,
\mathnormal{N}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\star}
, and its position
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\mathrm{G}}
, with
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{G}}
as a catch-all for any remaining parameters. All other things being equal, galaxies with more stars have more habitats in which intelligent life can evolve, increasing the mean galaxy-wide rate at which metasocieties appear. It takes time for intelligence to evolve, thus the cosmic epoch of the galaxy matters; distance also directly affects observables. There are many other factors that could affect the evolution and spread of ETIs: star formation history, metallicity distribution, stellar density and velocity dispersion, nuclear activity, and the nature of the interstellar medium all could be important (e.g.,
Gonzalez et al. 67
,
Lineweaver et al. 120
,
Dayal et al. 45
,
Di Stefano & Ray 46
,
Gowanlock 68
;
Balbi & Tombesi 12
,
Lingam et al. 121
;
Lacki 111
).

It is also possible the development of new metasocieties is affected by their predecessors, and thus there are techno-historical parameters for each galaxy as well. The science-fiction trope wherein relics of a long-dead ETI yield advanced technologies for practical interstellar travel (e.g., the “subway” system in
Sagan 152
) comes to mind – later ETIs would have an easier time spreading than the first ones. Or, early ETIs might instead launch self-replicating probes that actively destroy all new ETIs
[see
21
, and discussion therein]
. Even so, we might regard all these ETIs as being part of a single metasociety, albeit one whose societies are highly clustered in time, their appearances poorly described by a Poisson process (see Section
VI.3
).

Observational programs only draw a sample of the cosmic population. This sample is fixed in targeted observations typical of most extragalactic radio SETI efforts to date
[
162
,
70
]
. Large-scale cosmic surveys may be viewed as drawing random samples from the Universe
[
128
]
. Random samples of background galaxies are also present in targeted pointings at nearby objects
[
64
, see]
. When the galaxies being studied are already known, we conditionalize on the known galaxy sample; when treating galaxies statistically, there is a galactic contribution to the sampling variance.

V.3
Stars: A tracer of ETIs?

Planetary systems around stars are commonly regarded as the origin sites of ETIs. The properties of a sun can bear on whether a metasociety originates there.
27
27

27

H
Hypothetically, ETIs could originate elsewhere; rogue planets detached from any star may even be habitable
[
166
,
1
,
8
, e.g.,]
. On the whole, most baryonic bodies like planets should trace the stellar mass on large scales, modulated by other quantities, as discussed below.
The star needs to live long enough for intelligence to evolve, for one
[
90
,
24
,
125
]
, and its luminosity should be steady enough that a planet can remain habitable for geological times
[
83
,
99
]
. There is a continuing debate on whether planets around M dwarfs can develop life, against the backdrop of planetary tidal locking and large stellar flares (
Shields et al. 161
and references therein); the space weather around them might discourage radio communications if a society evolves
[
34
]
. Stellar parameters could also affect whether a planetary system is settled: mass and metallicity are correlated with number and types of attendant planets
[
94
,
134
, e.g.,]
; exotic types of stars might draw ETIs interested in astroengineering
[
54
,
116
,
28
,
158
,
143
,
91
,
110
,
123
]
; and so on. For a more precise, fine-grained treatment, we consider stellar systems instead of stars, with the distribution including quantities describing the architecture of the system: how many stars, in what orbits; the number, sizes, and locations of planets; their satellites; and other variables that could be relevant to whether there are inhabitants
[
177
, e.g.,]
.

Societies and their broadcasts may be concentrated in regions of high stellar density, without necessarily being hosted by stars. On a galactic scale, we may expect baryonic resources, stellar energy, and interstellar habitats like interstellar objects to trace the stellar mass distribution. These could be useful for ETIs in astroengineering projects. However, ETIs could migrate to the cores or outskirts of galaxies, driven by the possibility of more efficient collaboration
[
97
,
165
]
, more optimal thermodynamic environments
[
32
]
, or large-scale gradients in habitats. Some possible “attractor” objects trace young stars instead of total stellar mass, like high-mass X-ray binaries
[
176
, as in]
, maybe even concentrating ETIs into a few clumps while leaving the rest of the galaxy empty. Even if they roughly follow stars, the dependence need not be linear; they might be far more prevalent in regions of higher stellar density because interstellar travel is easier, for example
[
46
,
61
,
111
]
. In the absence of secure knowledge of the relevant tradeoffs, I posit that the density of societies in inhabited galaxies follows the stars on a large scale.

Although they do not fit neatly into the “tree” of objects, it is useful to treat stars as another class of objects with its own point process. The stars are points scattered in a “stellar haystack”
\mathnormal{W}^{\star}
, described by parameter tuples
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\star}
. Like other types of objects, windows select stars based on position in their haystack. Galaxies host stars, but stars might also be regarded as hosted by metasocieties and societies if their planetary system is home to an ETI.
28
28

28

F
Formally, we could use projections of the position and time of the “host” (meta)society to pick out stars (Section
III.2.1
). In any case, because societies are localized in single planetary systems or interstellar space,
\mathnormal{N}_{\mathrm{C}}^{\star}
is zero, one, or perhaps up to a few in multiple star systems, depending on whether we are considering stars
per se
or stellar systems.
Thus, we can consider the stellar sample
{\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\star}}
drawn by a selection
(\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax{\mathrm{J}})
, consisting of
{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\star}}
stars.

The stellar properties considered here are current position
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\star}
, birth time
\vartheta_{\star}
, and initial mass
\mathnormal{m}_{\star}
. Lifespan
\tau_{\star}
is regarded as a function of
\mathnormal{m}_{\star}
. The stars then have a distribution function

{\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\star}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\star}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}})}=\frac{d^{4}\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\star}}\right\rangle}{d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\star}d\vartheta_{\star}d\mathnormal{m}_{\star}d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}^{\prime}_{\star}}{(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\star}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}})}\mathchar 59\relax

(63)

a joint stellar density, initial mass function, and star formation history. Any other parameters are absorbed into
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}^{\prime}_{\star}
. This distribution can be used as a term in metasocietal, societal, or broadcast distribution to introduce a dependence on stellar properties.

VI
The ETIs themselves: metasocieties and societies

VI.1
Metasocieties

Intelligences are organized into metasocieties and societies. An inhabited galaxy contains one or more metasocieties at the present moment:
{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}(t)}\geq 1
. A metasociety is a collection of interacting ETI societies shaped by a shared history. A society and all of its “offspring” always are part of the same metasociety for our purposes here. The technosignatures of a metasociety have a shared influence from a common origin. Metasocieties, unlike individual societies, can be extended over galactic scales.

The nature and evolution of metasocieties is one of the most speculative areas of SETI, with direct bearing on the Fermi Paradox and expected technosignatures. A metasociety might consist of only one planet-confined society, a loose galactic network of worlds, an expanding interstellar settlement front, or a dense equilibrium state, among other possibilities (section
VI.3
).

Given the diversity of hypotheses, huge uncertainties, and the possible amplification of stochastic effects by initial exponential growth, there is no one obviously correct single set of parameters to describe them. I characterize them with these parameters:

•

All metasocieties are regarded as having a single origin, arising at a single well-defined time
\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{M}}
, at a single location. The origin site is indicated by a parameter tuple
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\star}^{\mathrm{M}}
describing the first inhabited host star (see section
V.3
). The full distribution includes a rate of metasociety appearance per star in an empty galaxy, even allowing us to establish a functional dependence on stellar properties. However, the number of current metasocieties does not necessarily scale with
\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\star}\right\rangle
if they are sufficiently common and cover the entire galaxy (section
VI.3
). Actual metasocieties may form as a fusion of smaller metasocieties
[
97
,
58
]
; the “origin” might be for the first or perhaps a dominant component, but maybe the origins of the others affect the result as well.

•

All metasocieties cover a finite volume
\mathnormal{v}_{\mathrm{M}}
containing a finite number of astrophysical objects centered at position
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\mathrm{M}}
. Not all regions with the same spatial volume are equivalent – maybe there are more societies in a volume that includes the core or disk of a galaxy than one that only includes the distant halo. An alternative is the mean number of stars covered by a metasociety,
\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\star}\right\rangle
. In the (perhaps simplistic) scenarios I discuss, however, the metasociety is either restricted to one stellar system with
\mathnormal{v}_{\mathrm{M}}\to 0
or pervades the entire galaxy. Conceivably, multiple metasocieties might overlap, perhaps inhabiting different habitats (e.g., planetary surfaces versus deep space versus compact objects).

•

The lifespan of the metasociety,
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{M}}
. Metasocieties may have effectively undefined lifespans, however, especially if they cover entire galaxies, continuously regenerating through internal replication.
29
29

29

Of course, in the very long run, all metasocieties will perish in our current understanding of cosmology, but there will be hydrogen-burning stars to host them for
10
trillion years
[
114
]
, much less more exotic remnants
[
2
]
.

•

The mean fraction of societies within the metasociety that are communicative,
\mathnormal{f}^{\mathrm{C}}_{\mathrm{M}}
.

•

Other parameters describing its internal distribution of communicative societies,
\mathnormal{\boldsymbol{w}}^{\mathrm{C}}_{\mathrm{M}}
, and metasociety-wide properties of broadcasts
\mathnormal{\boldsymbol{w}}^{\mathrm{B}}_{\mathrm{M}}
. Most obviously, this can include something that governs the number of communicative societies, like the rate of appearance of new societies per star. A metasociety necessarily has at least one society over its history, but the number of communicative societies may be zero if most societies are unable or unwilling to make broadcasts. For broadcasts, this could include a typical energy scale or typical rate per society. These are all lumped under a residual tuple,
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{M}}
.

Under the assumption that metasocieties are defined by these specific properties, the adopted metasocietal distribution has the form:

{\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{M}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{G}})}=\frac{d^{7}\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}}\right\rangle}{d\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{M}}d\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{M}}d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\mathrm{M}}d\mathnormal{v}_{\mathrm{M}}d\mathnormal{f}^{\mathrm{C}}_{\mathrm{M}}d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\star}^{\mathrm{M}}d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{M}}}{(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{M}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{G}})}.

(64)

Depending on scenario, metasocieties may interact in complicated ways, so the metasocietal point process
{\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{G}})}
can be far from Poissonian (see discussion in Section
VI.3
).

Although most of these parameters are regarded as single numbers, the framework is sufficiently general that they can be replaced with subtuples, expanding the haystack with more dimensions. Metasocietal size is treated here as a fixed value, if only as a placeholder because the volume does not come into play in this paper. While interstellar expansion can be quick on cosmological timescales, it still takes time and we could catch it in progress
[
190
]
. To account for that, we could replace “size” with parameters describing the velocity of the expansion front
[
95
]
, stellar diffusion parameters
[
23
]
, initial low filling factors due to percolation
[
112
]
, and so on. The origin star’s parameter tuple could be expanded to model the star’s trajectory through the galaxy, including its initial velocity and perhaps artificial propulsion
[
9
]
, if we regard its position as remaining important. The communicative fraction
\mathnormal{f}^{\mathrm{C}}_{\mathrm{M}}
could be replaced with parameters for a function describing communicativeness as a function of societal age
[
151
]
. Many such complications can be postulated, and the distribution altered to treat them.

VI.2
Societies

In this series, a society is considered a localized, independent entity with its infrastructure.
30
30

30

T
Though “society” may suggest distinct individuals cooperating toward common goals, the concept applies even if these (inter)planetary entities are solitary beings, group minds, ecospheres, or even large collections of noninteracting intelligences.
A communicating society is one that emits broadcasts. In this view, a “society” does not just include sentient beings, but the technology they produce. Thus, a society may produce broadcasts long after its inhabitants have perished; as many have noted, the longevity of technosignatures themselves determines the number of detectable ETIs (e.g.,
Carrigan 22
,
Balbi & Ćirković 11
;
Wright et al. 187
). Conversely, a communicating society may effectively disappear when it loses interest in broadcasting, even while the society itself thrives (
Sagan 151
;
Smart 165
).

Classically, SETI considers single planetary systems or worlds as unitary bodies, naturally imposing a discreteness. The society level could be much finer, though, including individual interstellar vehicles and inhabited interstellar objects, or distinct organizations on a single world. The most relevant factor here is the induced clumping of broadcasts; a vast number of interstellar transmitting entities could reduce the Poisson fluctuations of inhabited galaxies. However, there could plausibly be more ‘‘levels,’’ each imposing its discreteness effects, requiring more complex models.
31
31

31

What are called “civilizations” or “societies” in SETI are planetary metasocieties; all the diverse cultures, institutions, and peoples of a planet are amalgamated into one entity. This single entity could have a much longer lifespan than any society as sociologically defined. But a single world or planetary system can be far more tightly integrated than an entire galaxy, so there is motivation to treat them as one unit.

Each society starts at time
\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{C}}
and survives for a finite duration
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{C}}
. It is located at
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\mathrm{C}}
; when considering very long timescales, we can instead use parameters describing its trajectory. Thus I adopt

{\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{M}})}=\frac{d^{4}\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}}\right\rangle}{d\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{C}}d\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{C}}d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\mathrm{C}}d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{C}}}{(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{M}})}

(65)

as the societal distribution for a metasociety
{\mathrm{M}}
. Any other parameters, including those describing a society’s broadcast population, are collected into
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{C}}
. As with metasocieties, individual parameters listed here can be replaced with sets of parameters (e.g., describing the society’s trajectory instead of just its position). If a metasociety has more than one society, the appearance of those societies is assumed to be Poissonian. Furthermore, all the properties of the societies are assumed to be independent of one another, including their broadcast distributions.

Long-lived interstellar metasocieties are plausibly in an equilibrium state, motivating a single
\mathnormal{\Xi}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}
for each one. If ETIs tend to fill available habitats, then they can do so in much less than 1 Gyr. Sustainability arguments suggest that metasocieties necessarily have to self-regulate to avoid resource depletion
[
57
,
79
]
. The reality may be more complicated than a simple Poissonian equilibrium process, however, with many hierarchical levels of organization that this model neglects. Perhaps metasocieties are highly chaotic, with a turbulent series of spikes, booms, plateaus, and crashes. Metasocieties that consist of several submetasocieties knit together by messages in relics could be extremely intermittent. Percolation hypotheses suggest that metasocieties may be spatially inhomogeneous as well
[
112
]
, albeit subject to stellar mixing
[
189
]
and societal turnover
[
182
]
. Without interstellar travel, even a multitude of independent transmitting programs is “clumped” onto the rare broadcasting host worlds that presumably dominate the Poissonian fluctuations. More detailed models are necessary to treat effects like these.

VI.3
Scenarios for metasocietal evolution

VI.3.1
The “classical” scenario: metasocieties as societies

The default scenario in SETI theory is that societies are confined to one solar system, surviving for a continuous span of time before disappearing forever without replication
[
140
, e.g.,]
. Thus, each metasociety covers just one stellar system and contains just one society, which may or may not be communicative. Each (meta)society is regarded as appearing and behaving independently of the others. The adopted metasocietal distribution function is

{\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{M}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{G}})}=\updelta(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\mathrm{M}}-\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\star}^{\mathrm{M}})\updelta(\mathnormal{v}_{\mathrm{M}}){\uppsi\left[\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{M}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{f}^{\mathrm{C}}_{\mathrm{M}}\mathchar 59\relax\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{M}}\right]_{\mathrm{G}}}\\
\cdot~\breve{\mathnormal{\Gamma}}_{\star}^{\mathrm{M}}(\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{M}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\star}^{\mathrm{M}}){\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\star}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\star}^{\mathrm{M}}{|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{G}}}).

(66)

The metasociety thus has zero size (
\mathnormal{v}_{\mathrm{M}}=0
) and its position is identical to that of the star it originated from. The number of metasocieties is proportional to the stellar distribution, but it is modulated by the number rate of metasocieties appearing around each star at a given time after its birth. That term includes dependence on the stellar parameter tuple – factors like the star’s position in a galaxy, mass, and metallicity might all affect the prevalence of metasocieties. Note also that the delay-time distribution does not generally integrate to
1
; it is vastly less if ETIs are rare, but can be more than
1
if technological societies evolve several times in a given planetary system
[
185
,
156
]
.
32
32

32

A simple societal intensity distribution does not contain enough information to model the clustering of metasocieties that happens when they evolve multiple times on rare worlds (e.g., those with complex multicellular life). An additional level for inhabited worlds can be added to the tree to address that. Nor does equation
66
account for what happens if subsequent metasocieties around the same star overlap; it is presumed that their lifespans are much shorter than the gap time between them.

With only one society per metasociety, there is no coordination or dependence between different societies. Whether or not a metasociety’s single society is communicative is random:

{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{M}})}\sim\mathrm{Bern}({\mathnormal{f}^{\mathrm{C}}_{\mathrm{M}}})\mathchar 59\relax

(67)

with a probability
{\mathnormal{f}^{\mathrm{C}}_{\mathrm{M}}}
determined by
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{M}}
, the “communicative” factor in the Drake equation. The distribution of metasocieties (and societies) is entirely set by galactic properties. For a realized galaxy they are treated as a Poissonian process because of this independence
[
66
, as in]
:

\displaystyle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{G}})}\sim\mathrm{Pois}(\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}}\right\rangle)

\displaystyle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{C}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{G}})}\sim\mathrm{Pois}(\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{C}}}\right\rangle)\sim\mathrm{Pois}({\left\langle\mathnormal{f}^{\mathrm{C}}_{\mathrm{M}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}}\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}}\right\rangle)\mathchar 59\relax

(68)

where the marking and thinning of societies by their communicativeness preserves the Poisson character. Communicative societies inherit the lifespan and origin point of their parent metasociety, leading to a societal distribution

{\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{M}})}={\mathnormal{f}^{\mathrm{C}}_{\mathrm{M}}}\updelta(\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{C}}-{\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{M}}})\updelta(\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{C}}-{\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{M}}}){\updelta(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\mathrm{C}}-{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\mathrm{M}}})}{\uppsi\left[\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{C}}\right]_{\mathrm{M}}}.

(69)

As long as
{\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{M}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}}
is much shorter than the galaxy’s evolutionary timescales,

\displaystyle\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}}(t)\right\rangle\approx{\mathnormal{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}}(t){\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{M}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}}{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\star}(t)\right\rangle}

\displaystyle\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{C}}}(t)\right\rangle\approx{\mathnormal{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}}(t){\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{M}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}\left\langle\mathnormal{f}^{\mathrm{C}}_{\mathrm{M}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\star}(t)\right\rangle}.

(70)

Thus,
{\mathnormal{\Xi}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}\approx\mathnormal{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{M}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}}
. The rate and abundance themselves depend on factors like star formation rate and the delay-time distribution (Section
VI.4
).

VI.3.2
Galactic clubs as metasocieties

In the galactic club scenario, the first communicative society successfully contacts the succeeding ones through either remote transmissions or nonreplicating probes, establishing norms and protocols (
Bracewell 19
). The protocols become locked in, with each new communicative society conforming to the precedent set by the previous ones. The implicit coordination results in a single galaxy-spanning metasociety, even though the societies themselves remain planetbound, aside possibly from some automated probes. The classical scenario is a special kind of galactic club scenario; it simply interprets “metasociety” differently.

Much of the metasocietal distribution function is a formality. A metasociety’s size and position are simply those of the host galaxy. A galactic club is born with the first society, its origin star is the site of that society, and it endures for
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\infty}
, an arbitrary timescale longer than the current age of the galaxy. The number of metasocieties in a given host galaxy is a Bernoulli random variable, simply determined by the societies:
\mathnormal{P}({\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}(t)}=1)=\mathnormal{P}({\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{h}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{C}}(t)}\geq 1)
. The way the metasociety influences its children is through a single metasociety-wide
\mathnormal{f}^{\mathrm{C}}_{\mathrm{M}}
, and the distribution of any additional parameters in
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{M}}
describing shared societal and broadcast properties.
33
33

33

F
For example, perhaps each society broadcasts only at a frequency assigned by the metasociety according to some distribution. Or perhaps the “culture” of the galactic club can bias the lifespans of its member societies.

The galactic club metasociety is therefore largely an emergent phenomenon, considered here only as a mechanism to coordinate societal and broadcast properties. Societies are the fundamental entities driving the galactic club. Despite the seeming circular dependence between the metasociety and its hosted societies, in practice, the dependence is one-way for any single parameter. The births of societies are entirely independent of each other, so if we restrict the societal haystack to just
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\mathrm{C}}
and
\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{C}}
, a realized host galaxy’s population is a Poisson point process. Meanwhile, the metasociety’s
\mathnormal{f}^{\mathrm{C}}_{\mathrm{M}}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{M}}
are chosen and then used to mark each society with its other parameters. The societal haystack is then expanded into the remaining dimensions, and thinned according to which ones are communicative. Finally, the origin of the metasociety is identified with the first (communicative) society.

When we consider a host galaxy with fixed properties and conditionalize on any metasocietal parameters governing societal/broadcast distributions, the societies are modeled with a Poisson point process:

{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{C}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{G}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{M}})}\sim\mathrm{Pois}(\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{C}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{M}}}\right\rangle).

(71)

The societal distribution includes a dependence on the stellar distribution, because they originate around stars:

{\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{C}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{G}}\mathchar 59\relax\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{M}})}={\uppsi\left[\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{C}}\mathchar 59\relax\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{C}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{M}}\right]_{\mathrm{G}}}\\
\cdot~\int_{\mathnormal{W}^{\star}}\updelta(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\mathrm{C}}-{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\star}})\breve{\mathnormal{\Gamma}}_{\star}^{\mathrm{C}}(\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{C}}|{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\star}\mathchar 59\relax\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{M}}}){\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\star}}({\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\star}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{G}}\mathchar 59\relax\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{M}}})d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\star}.

(72)

Actual galactic metasocieties may be more complicated, even in the absence of interstellar travel.
Forgan [58]
argues that instead multiple ‘‘galactic cliques’’ would arise, although they may in turn contact each other and fuse through a long period of inter-negotiation. There may in fact be a patchwork of different technosignature footprints in a galaxy, on intermediate spatial scales.
34
34

34

This might also be true of expansive metasocieties.

VI.3.3
Expansive metasocieties

Common interstellar travel allows for galaxy-spanning metasocieties. In an expansive metasociety, the metasociety spreads throughout a galaxy in a (cosmologically) minuscule time, treated as instantaneous here.
35
35

35

I
If a galaxy is seeded with life by direct panspermia
[
40
]
, in turn evolving ETIs, it could be viewed as hosting an expansive metasociety that takes billions of years to develop.
The technosignature population of the galaxy effectively undergoes a “phase transition”
[
33
, see]
. Only one metasociety is allowed at a time in a galaxy. Like the galactic club, the size and position of the metasociety is that of the galaxy itself. A metasociety may persist once established as internal migration reestablishes societies in locations that have fallen, in which case we can set
{\uppsi\left[\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{M}}\right]_{\mathrm{G}}}=\updelta(\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{M}}-\mathnormal{\tau}_{\infty})
. Or, perhaps some internal process causes them to vanish in mere millions of years or less
[
79
,
146
, as in]
. Note this lifespan is likely far greater than that of individual societies; a galaxy-wide catastrophe is required for a galaxy-spanning metasociety to collapse.

Each metasociety arises from a single stellar system, but because only one metasociety exists at a time, the realized rate can have a complicated dependence. If a new society evolves independently in an already established metasociety, there are many possible outcomes: it could be assimilated into it without affecting its properties appreciably, it could “rejuvenate” a metasociety and extend its lifespan, or perhaps it could subsume the extant metasociety with its own. There could be different metasocieties coexisting, or aggressive metasocieties could inhibit the evolution of intelligence on all other planets in the galaxy, and so on. Each of these affects the metasocietal distribution in different ways. The key point is that this interaction induces a
dependent
thinning on the metasocietal point process.

A fairly simple subscenario is one where metasocieties are exclusive and inhibitory: once a metasociety appears, it persists without interference for its entire lifespan, then vanishes. No other metasociety can arise during its reign. This means
{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}(\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{M}}\mathchar 59\relax\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{G}})}
is a Bernoulli variable,
1
if the galaxy has a metasociety at
t=\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{M}}
and
0
if it is empty. To model these metasocieties, define an auxiliary distribution describing their appearance rate in the absence of inhibition, if
{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}(\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{M}})}
just happened to be
0
:

{\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}=0}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{M}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{G}})}=\updelta(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\mathrm{M}}-{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\mathrm{G}}})\updelta(\mathnormal{v}_{\mathrm{M}}-{\mathnormal{v}_{\mathrm{G}}}){\uppsi\left[\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{M}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{f}^{\mathrm{C}}_{\mathrm{M}}\mathchar 59\relax\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{M}}\right]_{\mathrm{G}}}\\
\cdot~\breve{\mathnormal{\Gamma}}_{\star}^{\mathrm{M}=0}(\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{M}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\star}^{\mathrm{M}}){\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\star}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\star}^{\mathrm{M}}{|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{G}}}).

(73)

with
\breve{\mathnormal{\Gamma}}_{\star}^{\mathrm{M}=0}=d{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{M}}|\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}(\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{M}})=0\right\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}}/d\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{M}}
. In an unpopulated galaxy, metasocieties originate around stars much like they do in the classical scenario. Without any interaction, their population is Poisson given the galaxy’s properties. Hence, there is again a stellar distribution function and a rate per star. This counterfactual distribution can be marginalized to get an effective rate of appearance
{\breve{\mathnormal{\Gamma}}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}=0}}\equiv d\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}|\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}(\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{M}})=0}\right\rangle/d\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{M}}
. If extant metasocieties have lifespans unaffected by any independent “second origins” in their domains, then the distribution function is

{\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{M}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{G}})}=(1-\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}(\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{M}})}\right\rangle){\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}=0}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{M}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{G}})}.

(74)

It is found by integrating
{\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}}
with origin time
\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{M}}
restricted by duration
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{M}}
(
t-\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{M}}\leq\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{M}}\leq t
; section
III.8
). Differentiating by
\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{M}}
turns this into a partial differential equation.

A few simple results are evident. When every expansive metasociety is persistent,
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{M}}\to\mathnormal{\tau}_{\infty}
. A galaxy then has an expansive metasociety if and only if one has ever appeared any time during its history. The only way it lacks one is if no society develops during any interval of time. Because the first society in a realized galaxy arises according to a Poisson process, the time until one appears has an exponential distribution governed by
{\mathnormal{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}=0}\equiv\breve{\mathnormal{\Gamma}}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}=0}/\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\star}(\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{M}})\right\rangle}
. The mean number of metasocieties is

\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}}(t)\right\rangle=1-\exp\left(-\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\star}}(t)\right\rangle{\mathnormal{\Xi}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}=0}}(t{|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{G}}})\right)\mathchar 59\relax

(75)

defined in terms of the effective abundance per star

{\mathnormal{\Xi}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}=0}}(t{|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{G}}})=\frac{1}{\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\star}}(t)\right\rangle}\int_{{\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{G}}}}^{t}{\breve{\mathnormal{\Gamma}}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}=0}}(\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{M}}{|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{G}}})d\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{M}}\mathchar 59\relax

(76)

in turn using the effective rate found by marginalizing
{\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}=0}}
.

But what if metasocieties are short-lived compared to the galaxy’s age? If
{\mathnormal{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}=0}}(\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{M}})
is roughly constant given a
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{G}}
, then the evolution should be described by

\frac{d\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}}{(t)}\right\rangle}{dt}\approx{\mathnormal{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}=0}}\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\star}}\right\rangle(1-\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}(t)}\right\rangle)-\frac{\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}(t)}\right\rangle}{{\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{M}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}}}\mathchar 59\relax

(77)

with the first term on the right side describing the modified appearance rate, and the second term describing their disappearance. The natural equilibrium number of metasocieties is

\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}(t)}\right\rangle=[1+({\mathnormal{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}=0}}\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\star}(t)}\right\rangle{\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{M}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}})^{-1}]^{-1}.

(78)

The convergence to
1
in equation
78
is slower because all galaxies start with
{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}(t)}=0
. If
{\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{M}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}}
is long on cosmological scales, then there has not been time to reach the second “gap” between metasocieties – each galaxy has either avoided having any or is in the era of its first metasociety. In contrast, if
{\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{M}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}}
is short, then each galaxy has already had a random sequence of metasocieties and gaps. Equation
78
thus describes the duty cycle of expansive metasocieties once the transient initial condition has vanished. I adopt equation
75
unless otherwise stated, but to order of magnitude the abundance of metasocieties should be the same.

Expansive metasocieties differ from galactic clubs in that the number of communicative societies saturates at a very high equilibrium level once they become established – potentially in the billions or more. We have a metasociety-level societal distribution of

{\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{M}})=\mathnormal{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}(\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{C}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{M}})}\uppsi\left[\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{C}}\mathchar 59\relax\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{C}}\right]_{\mathrm{M}}\\
{\frac{d\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\star}(\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{C}})}\right\rangle}{d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\star}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\mathrm{C}}{|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{M}}}).}

(79)

Furthermore,
{\mathnormal{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}}
is not only expected to be larger than but
independent
of
{\mathnormal{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}=0}}
. The number of communicative societies in an expansive metasociety is treated separately. They form a Poisson point process, dependent on the metasociety’s properties:

{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{M}})}\sim\mathrm{Pois}(\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}}\right\rangle)

(80)

with
\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}}\right\rangle\approx{\mathnormal{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{C}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{M}}\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\star}\right\rangle}
when
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}\ll\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{C}}
. This means
{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{C}}(t)}
has a mixture distribution, with a
(1-\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}{(t)}}\right\rangle)
probability of being
0
and a
\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}(t)}\right\rangle
probability of being
{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}(t)}
, yielding:

\mathnormal{P}({\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{C}}(t)}\geq 1)=\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}(t)}\right\rangle[1-\exp(-{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}(t)\right\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}})].

(81)

The independence results in interesting effects: in particular, when
\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}(t)}\right\rangle\ll 1
, the probability of detecting a broadcast can vary quadratically with the number of stars in a galaxy (Section
XII
).

The qualitative characteristics of galactic clubs and persistent expansive metasocieties converge when
\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}}\right\rangle
approaches
1
. In both cases, metasociety-wide traits can be treated as galactic traits and societies form a Poisson point process in a realized galaxy. A galactic club can even be regarded as an expansive metasociety that spreads through telecommunications instead of settlement, albeit with much lower population densities in the absence of replication. A single metasociety approximation, with
{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}}=1
, serves to work for both scenarios when ETIs are not very rare.

VI.4
The Drake Equation in the formalism

The Drake equation applies to the classical scenario when the galaxy is in a steady state
[
50
,
66
]
. New stars are formed at a constant rate, and as they age over billions of years, some fraction of those host single ETI societies of limited lifespan. To implement it simplistically, if an ETI evolves, it must appear exactly
\overline{\mathnormal{t}}
after the star’s birth: the delay-time distribution is

\breve{\mathnormal{\Gamma}}_{\star}^{\mathrm{M}}=\mathnormal{f}_{\star}^{\mathrm{M}}\updelta((\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{M}}-\vartheta_{\star})-\overline{\mathnormal{t}}).

(82)

Stars need to maintain a stable luminosity long enough for intelligence to evolve on Earth-like planets, excluding massive stars. However, let us imagine for simplicity that all stars with
\mathnormal{m}_{\star}\leq\mathnormal{m}_{\star}^{H}
are equally likely to host an ETI. This probability
\mathnormal{f}_{\star}^{\mathrm{M}}
includes all terms in the Drake equation relating to the number of planets, habitability, and the evolution of life and intelligence. Then we can apply the above to the classical metasociety distribution (equation
73
).

We want to calculate the number of communicative societies existing at any one time in the galaxy. First, let us calculate the galactic-level societal distribution, using equation
15
. We convolve
\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{M}})
with
{\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{M}}{|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{G}}})
. Directly plugging these into that equation yields a formidable integral over all stellar and metasocietal parameters. Actually, most of the variables are effectively nuisance parameters, and the expression is simplified by the many delta functions and the separable integrals. In the end, we are left with

{\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{C}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{G}})}={\left\langle\mathnormal{f}_{\star}^{\mathrm{M}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}}{\left\langle\mathnormal{f}^{\mathrm{C}}_{\mathrm{M}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}\uppsi\left[\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\star}^{\mathrm{M}}\right]_{\mathrm{G}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\mathrm{C}})\uppsi\left[\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{M}}\right]_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{C}})}\\
\cdot~{\uppsi\left[\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{C}}\right]_{\mathrm{G}}}{\breve{\mathnormal{\Gamma}}^{\star}_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{M}}-\overline{\mathnormal{t}})}F[\mathnormal{m}_{\star}^{\mathrm{M}}](\mathnormal{m}_{\star}^{H}).

(83)

In other words, the communicative societies trace the stars in space, each existing for the lifespan of its metasociety, and they appear at a rate proportional to the star formation rate
\overline{\mathnormal{t}}
ago, including only stars that last long enough for ETIs to evolve.

The expected number of currently active communicative societies
\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{C}}}(t)\right\rangle
is the integral of
{\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{C}}}
with
t-\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{C}}\leq\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{C}}\leq t
(section
III.8
). Now let us suppose that the star formation rate of the galaxy has been constant, that (meta)societies are always much younger than the galaxy, and that
F[\mathnormal{m}_{\star}^{\mathrm{M}}](\mathnormal{m}_{\star}^{H})\approx 1
because most stars are low mass and likely to survive for the required billions of years. Then we finally get,

\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{C}}}(t)\right\rangle\approx{\breve{\mathnormal{\Gamma}}^{\star}_{\mathrm{G}}}{\left\langle\mathnormal{f}_{\star}^{\mathrm{M}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}}{\left\langle\mathnormal{f}^{\mathrm{C}}_{\mathrm{M}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{M}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}}.

(84)

This is the Drake equation rewritten in the formalism.

Including different delay-time distributions, galactic habitability, dependence on stellar mass, and other effects is fairly straightforward. We simply change the
d{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\star}^{\mathrm{M}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}}/d\upvartheta^{\star}_{\mathrm{M}}
distribution to include dependencies on these parameters of the host star. A spread in delay times is necessary when treating quiescent galaxies where the star formation rate was quenched about
10
billion years ago.

VII
Broadcasts: General considerations

A broadcast is an artificial release of energy at a discrete site through some specified time and frequency range (see Tables
6
–
8
for notation). Not every technosignature is a broadcast: unpowered artifacts in the solar system (
Freitas & Valdes 60
;
Rose & Wright 149
;
Davies & Wagner 44
) and anomalous atmospheric compositions resulting from industrial pollution
[
180
,
119
]
are not broadcasts. On the other hand, not every broadcast need be an attempt at communication – they include directed energy transmission for power beaming
[
92
,
16
]
, propulsion
[
122
]
, or remote sensing
[
154
]
. Broadly speaking, waste heat from megastructures
[
53
]
and exhaust radiation from vehicles
[
81
]
are broadcasts too.

The modulation of starlight by astronomical-scale megastructures (
Arnold 6
,
Chennamangalam et al. 28
;
Wright et al. 186
;
Zackrisson et al. 191
;
Lacki 109
,
Suazo et al. 167
) is an interesting case – here, the technosignature is the blocking of an energy release that normally would happen. These might be viewed as negative energy broadcasts. Searches for galactic-scale obscuration of starlight
[
4
,
190
]
use a collective bound as in Paper II, much as waste heat searches look for the positive energy release from the reprocessing of this missing starlight.

VII.1
Basic parameters to describe broadcasts

The basic considerations for whether a broadcast is detectable are where it is, how bright it becomes, when it happens, and how it behaves. The first question is answered by the broadcast position
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\mathrm{B}}
.

Fundamentally, the brightness is controlled by the total energy released
\mathnormal{\epsilon}_{\mathrm{B}}
and its distribution into the transmitter’s sky over solid angle
\mathnormal{\omega}_{\mathrm{B}}
(refer to Table
8
for emission-related variables). These can be considered separately, but I use the total effective isotropic energy
\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}\equiv 4\pi d\mathnormal{\epsilon}_{\mathrm{B}}/d\mathnormal{\omega}_{\mathrm{B}}
of the broadcast to describe the brightness, evaluated toward the direction of the observer. In the absence of beaming,
\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}=\mathnormal{\epsilon}_{\mathrm{B}}
. When broadcasts are beamed, the
\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}
distribution has a large peak near zero for off-axis broadcasts. Yet even on-axis beamed broadcasts may have a wide range of
\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}
because of different beaming angles or simply different
\mathnormal{\epsilon}_{\mathrm{B}}
. Previous SETI works have essentially considered two basic classes of
\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}
distributions – the monoenergetic
\updelta
-distribution with a characteristic
\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}
and power-law distributions (e.g.,
Drake et al. 48
;
Gulkis 72
,
Dreher 52
). Both narrow and broad distributions seem plausible at this point, with the former favored by possible engineering constraints on deliberate transmissions and the latter motivated by the possible diversity of ETI capabilities and goals. Each broadcast’s emission may also have distinct degrees and states of polarization. The polarization properties are encapsulated in
\mathnormal{\varpi}_{\mathrm{B}}
, which should be understood as a Stokes vector or similar representation.

Surveys are sensitive to particular kinds of time-frequency properties – spectral lines or pulses, for example. Although the time-frequency behavior may be endlessly complex, I model it with five basic parameters. Broadcasts are limited in time, with a source-frame duration
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{B}}
, and in frequency, with a constant source-frame instantaneous bandwidth
\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{t}|\mathrm{B}}
. Broadcasts also start at times
\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{B}}
and are centered on source-frame frequencies
\mathnormal{\upsilon}_{\mathrm{B}}
. Finally, a drift rate
\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}
describes the skewness of the broadcast in the time-frequency space. The broadcast is entirely contained in the time range
[\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{B}}+\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{B}}]
and frequency range
[\mathnormal{\upsilon}_{\mathrm{B}}-\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathrm{B}}/2\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\upsilon}_{\mathrm{B}}+\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathrm{B}}/2]
, where the total bandwidth
\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathrm{B}}
is derived from
\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{t}|\mathrm{B}}
and
\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}
(
\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathrm{B}}=\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{t}|\mathrm{B}}+\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{B}}|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|
when they are constant).

These quantities define a haystack in which broadcasts are scattered. Most of them match the quantities defining haystacks in
Tarter [171]
and
Wright et al. [188]
. A separate modulation parameter is missing here, not being directly relevant for energy or photon detection, but bandwidth and drift rate both can be considered kinds of modulation. The main differences are in the treatment of time, reduced to a single quantity in previous works.
Wright et al. [188]
interpret this as a repetition period, while the duty cycle and the longevity of the transmitter are ignored. Including periodicity would necessitate adding at least one more dimension to the ones here (see discussion in Section
IV.1
). Bursts repeating at random intervals
[
104
]
, however, require no new dimensions – the mean rate is simply considered a property of the hosting society.

VII.2
Selection of broadcasts

When is a broadcast sampled? Primary considerations are whether a broadcast “touches” the window in physical spacetime (Section
III.8
) and frequency, with a “cross section” set by a duration and bandwidth. A broadcast has an effective bandwidth
\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{x}{:}t|\mathrm{B}}
during window
{\mathsf{x}}
, a quantity accounting for a broadcast’s spectral evolution. In some cases, the broadcast’s spectrum is unchanging (like the box model; section
IX
), in which case we can simply use
\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{x}{:}t|\mathrm{B}}={\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{t}|\mathrm{B}}}
. For the purposes of selection, I adopt

\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{x}{:}t|\mathrm{B}}=\diam\left\{\nu\Big|{\frac{d^{2}\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}}{dtd\nu}}(t\mathchar 59\relax\nu|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}})>0\right\}

(85)

where
\diam\mathnormal{A}={\max[}|a_{1}-a_{2}|\,|a_{1}\mathchar 59\relax a_{2}\in\mathnormal{A}{]}
.
36
36

36

\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{x}{:}t|\mathrm{B}}
and
\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}
are distinct – the latter accounts for the frequency, sky field, and so on as well in restricting the bandwidth. A broadcast at a frequency never covered by
{\mathsf{x}}
has
\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}{=0}
and a drifting signal that just barely “touches”
{\mathsf{x}}
has
\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\ll\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}
, but neither situation affects
\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{x}{:}t|\mathrm{B}}
. In all cases,
\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathrm{B}}\geq\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{x}{:}t|\mathrm{B}}\geq\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}
.

I regard the selection of a broadcast by window
{\mathsf{x}}
as a binary decision based on its time and frequency properties:

\mathsf{P}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\mathrm{B}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}})=\mathbb{I}[-\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{B}}\leq\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{B}}-\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{x}}\leq\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}]\\
\cdot~\mathbb{I}[|\mathnormal{\upsilon}_{\mathrm{B}}-\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}|\leq(1/2)(\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{x}{:}t|\mathrm{B}}+\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}})]

(86)

The presumption is that the broadcast’s ancestors also are chosen by
{\mathsf{x}}
, as required (Section
III.2.3
), because if the broadcast is active at some time and place, so are all its hosts – so
{\mathsf{x}}
must not impose any filters on characteristics specific to society, metasociety, or galaxy. Polarization characteristics are not considered, because of the likely crosstalk between the broadcast’s polarization and the observed polarizations, at least for linear polarization.

VII.3
Energy and photons emitted by broadcasts

A broadcast is detected through its emission (Table
8
). This can be measured in various forms, including energy and photons. Each broadcast has an (effective isotropic) energy output per polarization per unit time and frequency

\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\nu\mathchar 59\relax p;\mathrm{B}}(t\mathchar 59\relax\nu\mathchar 59\relax p)\equiv\frac{d^{2}\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{p;\mathrm{B}}}{dtd\nu}(t\mathchar 59\relax\nu\mathchar 59\relax p)=\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{p}|\mathrm{B}}(p)\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\nu;\mathrm{B}}(t\mathchar 59\relax\nu).

(87)

I assume that the degree and state of polarization are independent of time and frequency. Thus, I separate
\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\nu\mathchar 59\relax p;\mathrm{B}}
into the fraction of energy emitted in polarization
p
,
\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{p}|\mathrm{B}}(p)
, and the spectral luminosity,
\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\nu;\mathrm{B}}
.
37
37

37

For example, a broadcast with linear polarization fraction
\mathnormal{\varpi}_{\mathrm{lin};\mathrm{B}}
has
\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{p}|\mathrm{B}}(p_{\mathrm{lin}})=(1/2)(1-\mathnormal{\varpi}_{\mathrm{lin};\mathrm{B}})+\mathnormal{\varpi}_{\mathrm{lin};\mathrm{B}}\cos^{2}{\phi_{p_{\mathrm{lin}};\mathrm{B}}}
, where
{\phi_{p_{\mathrm{lin}};\mathrm{B}}}
is the relative angle between broadcast’s polarization angle and
p_{\mathrm{lin}}
.
The same basic ideas can be applied to broadcasts in other messengers, like neutrinos and gravity waves.

Of course, we do not collect all of the energy emitted by a broadcast, only that which falls on our instruments within a time and frequency window in set of observed polarizations. Out of a total effective isotropic emission
\mathring{\mathnormal{\varrho}}_{\mathrm{B}}
, the broadcast releases only a limited amount
\mathring{\mathnormal{\varrho}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}
within these constraints, zero if the broadcast is not in the sample. The amount of energy released within the time, frequency, and polarization constraints for
{\mathsf{x}}
is

\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}=\int_{\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}-\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}/2}^{\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}+\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}/2}\int_{\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{x}}}^{\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{x}}+\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}}\sum_{p\in\mathsf{\Pi}_{\mathsf{x}}}\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\nu\mathchar 59\relax p;\mathrm{B}}(t\mathchar 59\relax\nu\mathchar 59\relax p)dtd\nu

(88)

When the polarization properties are independent of time and frequency, the sum over polarizations is replaced by a single factor
\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}
, such that

{\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}=\sum_{p\in\mathsf{\Pi}_{\mathsf{x}}}\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{p}|\mathrm{B}}(p|\mathnormal{\varpi}_{\mathrm{B}}).}

(89)

Note that the frequency and time variables are all in the
source frame
.
38
38

38

An object at redshift
{z}
is observed at Earth to have
\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}=\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\oplus}(1+{z})
,
\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}=\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\oplus}(1+{z})
, and
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}=\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\oplus}/(1+{z})
.

When dealing with photon detectors, the relevant quantity is the number of photons emitted within the observational window:

\mathring{\mathnormal{q}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\equiv\int_{\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}-\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}/2}^{\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}+\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}/2}\int_{\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{x}}}^{\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{x}}+\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}}\sum_{p\in\mathsf{\Pi}_{\mathsf{x}}}\frac{\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\nu\mathchar 59\relax p;\mathrm{B}}(t\mathchar 59\relax\nu\mathchar 59\relax p)}{h\nu}dtd\nu\mathchar 59\relax

(90)

where
h
is Planck’s constant.

Even though the time domain and frequency domain amplitudes are related by the Fourier transform,
\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\nu;\mathrm{B}}
can have extremely complex dependence on time and frequency.
39
39

39

Formally,
\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathrm{B}}
and
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{B}}
cannot both be finite; applying a rectangular window to one domain convolves the other by a
\sinc
function that is nonzero over an infinite range. Many observations integrate over
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{o}}\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{o}}\gg 1
, resulting in negligible leakage. These include all practical observations in the optical or higher energies, and “filterbank” data products in radio SETI which also employ polyphase filterbank techniques to reduce sidebands
[
147
]
.
This motivates the later use of the box and chord models as simplifications.

.

VII.4
Distances, fluences, and dilutions

Observable quantities depend not just on the intrinsic properties of the broadcast, but where it is – its distance (
{d}_{\mathrm{B}}
) and position in the sky (
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{\mathrm{B}}
). The difference in redshift between broadcasts in the same galaxy is insignificant, so I set them equal (
{z}_{\mathrm{B}}={z}_{\mathrm{G}}
). Variation in broadcast distances is important for the Milky Way, which after all is another galaxy with some population of broadcasts (even if it is empty aside from our own). For extragalactic systems that are not Galactic satellites, however, the size of the target galaxy is negligible compared to its distance, and we can approximate the distance as that to the galaxy itself (
{d}_{\mathrm{B}}={d}_{\mathrm{G}}
).

Aside from the cuts imposed by the selections, only the emission incident on our detector can be measured. The fluence
\mathfrak{u}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}
of a broadcast is the amount of emission per unit area integrated over all combinations of time, frequency, and polarization covered by window
{\mathsf{x}}
. Specific types of fluence include the energy fluence
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}
where the emission is quantified as energy and the photon fluence
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}
which counts the expected number of photons (Table
8
).

The distance determines the flux and fluence of a broadcast with a given amount of emission. The energy fluence can be found using the “energy distance”
{d}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}=\sqrt{1+{z}}{d}_{M}
which accounts for redshift, correcting the transverse angular distance
{d}_{M}
(see
Hogg 87
for how to calculate
{d}_{M}
). Likewise, the photon fluence uses a “photon distance”
{d}_{\mathnormal{q}}
equal to
{d}_{M}
.

The observed emission is also partly blocked by opacity from dust and gas on the sightline, plus the Earth’s atmosphere, a range-limiting issue in optical SETI
[
89
]
. Only a fraction
{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}
of
\mathnormal{\varrho}
emission remains after absorption and scattering, the transmittance factor, which can vary with both position and frequency. The frequency dependence means that windows at different frequencies have different transmittances. At the usual radio frequencies observed in SETI, however,
{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}
is very near
1
.

It is convenient to express the inverse-square law and redshift effects as a “dilution factor,”
{y}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathrm{B}}=1/(4\pi{{d}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathrm{B}}}^{2})
. For emission of type
\mathnormal{\varrho}
,

\mathfrak{u}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}=\frac{\mathring{\mathnormal{\varrho}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}}}{4\pi{{d}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathrm{B}}}^{2}}=\mathring{\mathnormal{\varrho}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}}{y}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathrm{B}}.

(91)

In principle, the
\mathring{\mathnormal{\varrho}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}
and
{y}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathrm{B}}
are not independent – although the intrinsic properties of the broadcast are modeled as independent, the cuts depend on
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}
and
\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}
, which are affected by redshift. Even the Doppler shifts of broadcasts should have a slight effect on
\mathring{\mathnormal{\varrho}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}
. Transmittance, when it is not near
1
, is likely to be much lower in regions of high obscuration and thus is certainly not independent of
{y}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathrm{B}}
. However, for practical purposes, with the emission and dilution independent,

{\left\langle{\mathfrak{u}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}}^{n}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}=\left\langle{\mathring{\mathnormal{\varrho}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}}\vphantom{\mathring{\mathnormal{\varrho}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}}^{n}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}\left\langle{{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}}^{n}}{{y}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathrm{B}}}^{n}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}

(92)

for any arbitrary exponent
n
.

VIII
Populations of broadcasts

VIII.1
The broadcast distribution function

In this series, the broadcast distribution function takes the form

{\mathnormal{{\Psi}}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}})}=\frac{d^{8}\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle}{d\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}d\mathnormal{\varpi}_{\mathrm{B}}d\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{B}}d\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{B}}d\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{t}|\mathrm{B}}d\mathnormal{\upsilon}_{\mathrm{B}}d\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}{d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\mathrm{B}}}}{(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{J}})}\mathchar 59\relax

(93)

using the variables identified in Section
VII.1
. The broadcast population is a Cox point process, and the broadcast population of a realized society in particular is a Poisson point process. The instantaneous rate of broadcasts per frequency per star is especially useful:

{\mathnormal{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}(t\mathchar 59\relax\nu)\equiv{\frac{1}{\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\star}}(t)\right\rangle}\frac{d^{2}\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle}{d\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{B}}d\mathnormal{\upsilon}_{\mathrm{B}}}(\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{B}}=t\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\upsilon}_{\mathrm{B}}=\nu)}{\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}}

(94)

as is the frequency abundance per star,

{\mathnormal{Z}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}(t\mathchar 59\relax\nu)={\frac{1}{\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\star}}(t)\right\rangle}\frac{d\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}(t)\right\rangle}{d\mathnormal{\upsilon}_{\mathrm{B}}}(\mathnormal{\upsilon}_{\mathrm{B}}=\nu).}

(95)

The stellar rate
{\mathnormal{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}
and the stellar abundance
{\mathnormal{\Xi}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}
are additionally marginalized over
\mathnormal{\upsilon}_{\mathrm{B}}
, as defined as in Section
III.2.4
. It is more convenient to use the total abundances and rates, the derivatives without dependence on
\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\star}(\nu)}\right\rangle
, when considering individual societies around single star systems:
{\breve{\mathnormal{\Lambda}}_{\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\equiv d^{2}\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle/d\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{B}}d\mathnormal{\upsilon}_{\mathrm{B}}
,
{\breve{\mathnormal{Z}}_{\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\equiv d\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}(t)}\right\rangle/d\mathnormal{\upsilon}_{\mathrm{B}}
,
{\breve{\mathnormal{\Gamma}}_{\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\equiv d\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\upnu\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}(\nu)}\right\rangle/d\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{B}}
,
{\breve{\mathnormal{\Xi}}_{\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\equiv\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\cap\upnu\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}(t\mathchar 59\relax\nu)}\right\rangle
.

Specific examples of the broadcast distribution are given in Sections
IX
and
X
.

VIII.2
Interchangeability

When I compute properties of aggregate observables in this paper, I apply results for compound Poisson random variables. However, to do this, the variables being summed need to be identically distributed. Basically, each society is assumed to have the “same” broadcast distribution as the others, an “average” distribution. Likewise, each metasociety has the “same” societal and broadcast distributions as any other in the galaxy. The
interchangeability
assumption is that all distributions of an object type are the same, except for translations in spacetime (because objects are located in different places and start at different times). The broadcast distribution in every society (regardless of metasociety) in galaxy
{\mathrm{G}}
has the form:

\mathnormal{{\Psi}}_{\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}{(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}})}=f_{{\mathrm{G}}}^{\mathrm{B}}(\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\varpi}_{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{t}|\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\upsilon}_{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}})\\
\cdot\updelta(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\mathrm{B}}-\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\mathrm{C}})\cdot~\mathbb{I}[0\leq\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{B}}-\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{C}}\leq\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{C}}]\mathchar 59\relax

(96)

and every metasociety has a societal distribution

\mathnormal{\Psi}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}{(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}}|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{M}})}=f_{{\mathrm{G}}}^{\mathrm{C}}(\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{C}})\cdot\mathbb{I}[0\leq\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{B}}-\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{C}}\leq\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{C}}]\mathchar 59\relax

(97)

with fixed functions
f_{{\mathrm{G}}}^{\mathrm{B}}
and
f_{{\mathrm{G}}}^{\mathrm{C}}
.

Societies are selected at different points of their lifecycles, but for the compound Poisson distribution to be applicable, the societal broadcast distribution must be time-stationary. If, for example, societies lose interest in communicating as they age, then young societies have a higher
{\mathnormal{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}}
than older ones. Interchangeability does not allow this. Thus there can be no further dependence on
\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{B}}-\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{C}}
in equation
96
, as long as the broadcast is within bounds. Likewise, the societal distribution is stationary. More generally, there cannot be any edge effects wherein societies or metasocieties spend different amounts of time in the selection window; thus all lifespans must either be much longer or much shorter than the window’s duration.

Real societies and metasocieties may be incredibly diverse, each with its own unique array of technosignatures. Societies and broadcasts are represented by Cox processes (Section
II.5
). Because these are generally nonergodic, a single society or metasociety is a very poor representative of the ensemble properties of the underlying distribution – we only see one realization of the distribution, which itself is a random variable. If half of societies broadcast in radio and the rest in gamma rays, and there is only a single society that happens to be radio-broadcasting, we will not be able to “see” the true diversity in broadcasts. The observed sample is thus subjected to stochasticity when there are only a few societies. A large sample of societies can sample the entire gamut of broadcasts. In that sense, all the broadcasts can be pooled together into a galactic broadcast distribution, although the effects of the “clumping” remain underestimated by the compound Poisson distribution.

There are expressions for the mean and variances when dealing with diverse populations, but they are very complicated because of the nesting selection-relative averages and variances (equation
45
). It is more insightful to work directly with the distributions themselves when they are available (section
VIII.6
).

Table
9
lists equations for some important quantities characterizing broadcast populations, given the assumptions of independence and interchangeability.

VIII.3
The number of broadcasts

The number of broadcasts intercepted by
{\mathsf{x}}
from a society
{\mathrm{C}}\in{\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{C}}}
is
\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}})
, a Poisson random variable given
\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}}
. Its mean is given by the number of broadcasts that “touch” the
{\mathsf{x}}
window:

\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle=\idotsint_{\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}-(\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{x}{:}t|\mathrm{B}}+\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}})/2}^{\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}+(\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{x}{:}t|\mathrm{B}}+\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}})/2}\int_{\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{x}}-\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{B}}}^{\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{x}}+\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}}\\
{\mathnormal{{\Psi}}_{\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}{|\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}}})d\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{B}}d\mathnormal{\upsilon}_{\mathrm{B}}d\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}d\mathnormal{\varpi}_{\mathrm{B}}d\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{B}}d\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{t}|\mathrm{B}}d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\mathrm{B}}d\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}.

(98)

If societies are long-lived compared to
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{B}}
, and if
{\mathnormal{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}}
varies slowly enough in time and frequency compared to common values of
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{B}}
and
\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{x}{:}t|\mathrm{B}}
,

\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle\approx{\breve{\mathnormal{\Lambda}}_{\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}}(\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{x}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}){\cdot}(\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}+{\left\langle\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{x}{:}t|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{C}}})(\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}+{\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{C}}}){.}

(99)
