\left\langle{\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle

\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{C}}

\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{C}}

\mathbb{V}\left[{\mathnormal{K}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right]

\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle\left[\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{C}}+\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{C}}^{2}\right]

\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle\left[\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{C}}+\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{C}}^{2}\left(\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle+\left(1-\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}}\right\rangle\right)\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle\right)\right]

Note. — These expressions are calculated under the assumptions of independence and interchangeability. Note that
{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{L}}}\equiv{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}^{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{L}}}
and
{\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{K}}}\equiv{\left\langle\mathnormal{\kappa}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}}
by convention (section
III.4.2
).

The number of broadcasts is a compound random sum, with means and variances given in the table. As long as
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{B}}\ll\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{C}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{M}}
and
{\mathnormal{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{B}}}
is slowly varying,

\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle\approx{\mathnormal{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{B}}}(\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{x}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}){\cdot\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\star}\right\rangle}(\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}+{\left\langle\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{x}{:}t|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}})\\
\cdot(\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}+{\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}}).

(100)

It is scaled by the mean rate that broadcasts occur per unit frequency per star, which is

{\mathnormal{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{B}}}=\begin{cases}{\mathnormal{\Xi}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{C}}\left\langle\breve{\mathnormal{\Lambda}}_{\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}}&\text{(Galactic club)}\\
{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathnormal{\Xi}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}\left\langle\breve{\mathnormal{\Lambda}}_{\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}}&\text{(Expansive interstellar)}\end{cases}

(101)

This abundance is a key quantity in SETI. The “clumping” into societies and metasocieties adds additional sampling variance, more so in the expansive interstellar scenario where
{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}}
is independent of
\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}
. When fewer than one broadcast is typically selected per society, the probability that one broadcast is intercepted is

P({\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\geq 1)\approx\begin{cases}1-\exp(-\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle)\\
\hfill\text{(Galactic club)}\\
\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}}\right\rangle[1-\exp(-\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle)]\\
\hfill\text{(Expansive interstellar)}\end{cases}.

(102)

In the opposite limit of many selected broadcasts per society, this probability is simply the probability that one communicative society is selected. The variance in the number of broadcasts per metasociety is then mainly from the Poissonian clumping into societies, approaching
\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle^{2}
(full expressions are given in Table
9
).

VIII.4
Aggregate emission and fluence

The total single-polarization spectral luminosity from the broadcasts in a sample
{\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}
is

{\mathring{\mathnormal{L}}_{\nu\mathchar 59\relax p;\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}(t\mathchar 59\relax\nu\mathchar 59\relax p)={\sum_{\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{B}}\in\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}}\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\nu\mathchar 59\relax p;\mathrm{B}}(t\mathchar 59\relax\nu\mathchar 59\relax p)

(103)

Of course,
{\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}
itself is random, even when
{\mathrm{J}}
itself is specified. The usual conditions for broadcast selection by the window
{\mathsf{x}}
(equation
86
) define the bounds of integration to be used in equation
41
. Under the interchangeability-independence assumptions,

\left\langle{\mathring{\mathnormal{L}}_{\nu\mathchar 59\relax p;\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}(t\mathchar 59\relax\nu\mathchar 59\relax p)\right\rangle\approx{\mathnormal{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}(t\mathchar 59\relax\nu){\cdot\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\star}\right\rangle}{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\nu\mathchar 59\relax p;\mathrm{B}}(t\mathchar 59\relax\nu\mathchar 59\relax p)\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}}\\
\cdot~(\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}+{\left\langle\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{x}{:}t|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}})(\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}+{\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}).

(104)

Strictly speaking, this is not necessarily a compound Poisson variable because
{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{G}})}
is Bernoulli in the expansive metasociety scenario. It reaches the compound Poisson limit when
\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}}\right\rangle=1
and the galaxy as a whole can be identified with the metasociety (Table
9
).

The aggregate fluence is a similar compound sum under these assumptions. A fluence
{\mathfrak{U}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}
of any type from the sample is found by summing fluences
\mathfrak{u}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}
of the same type from individual broadcasts in a sample. Examples include the total energy fluence
{\mathfrak{H}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}
and total photon fluence
{\mathfrak{G}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}
of a sample, composed of the energy fluences
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}
and photon fluences
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}
of individual broadcasts. We have:

\left\langle{\mathfrak{U}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle\approx{\mathnormal{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}(\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{x}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}})}{\cdot\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\star}\right\rangle}(\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}+{\left\langle\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{x}{:}t|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}})\\
{\cdot(\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}+{\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}})\left\langle\mathfrak{u}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}.

(105)

The clumping of broadcasts into societies can have a big effect on the variance of
{\mathfrak{U}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}
(Table
9
). When a window typically samples
\ll 1
broadcast per society or the variance in
\mathfrak{u}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}
is sufficiently large, the variance in an inhabited galaxy
{\mathrm{G}}
with
{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}}=1
approaches what we would expect if
{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{B}}}
were Poissonian. In the opposite limit,
\mathbb{V}\left[{\mathfrak{U}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right]\approx\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathfrak{u}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{C}}^{2}=\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{C}}}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathfrak{U}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle^{2}
. The societies become a population of “standard candles,” and the fluctuations are just shot noise in their number. This limit requires many broadcasts per society just within a window, all becoming confused, which can be a tall order (Paper II).

VIII.4.1
What do we expect the aggregate emission to look like?

Although we usually envision broadcasts to resemble isolated “spikes” in
\mathring{\mathnormal{L}}_{\nu}
, on coarse enough time-frequency scales, this roughness blurs out. The mean spectrum, as shown by equation
104
, is proportional to
{\mathnormal{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{B}}}
itself, as long as it varies slowly enough and a mean
\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\nu\mathchar 59\relax p;\mathrm{B}}
exists. If there is no “magic frequency”
[
51
,
17
, unlike]
or “magic time”
[
144
,
127
,
38
,
139
, unlike]
that ETIs in the target galaxy prefer to broadcast at,
{\mathnormal{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{B}}}
is most likely a smooth function, perhaps a power law.

Supposing that the
\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}
distribution has a mean, over large bandwidths and long durations the aggregate emission converges to a constant luminosity source with a smooth spectrum. In other words, it will resemble diffuse nonthermal emission commonly seen in objects like active galactic nuclei. Like any population of discrete sources
[
174
, e.g.,]
, there are Poissonian fluctuations in the emission, far greater than those from the immense number of particles contributing to natural diffuse emission. With enough broadcasts being added together, the fluctuations also become Gaussian by the central limit theorem. This motivates use of the total emission of galaxies to set constraints on broadcasts, as in Paper II.

If the
\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}
distribution has no mean, or if broadcasts are so rare that confusion does not occur for any feasible sample, the aggregate emission remains “spiky,” with strongly non-Gaussian fluctuations that could be picked out by conventional search strategies. Nonetheless, there still could be a background of fainter broadcasts for which aggregate constraints still apply.

Even if there is a magic frequency, the broadcasts from an extended metasociety may be smeared out in received frequency because of velocity differences between the different transmitter sites. Then the aggregate emission will appear much like natural line emission, although possibly at a frequency corresponding to no natural transition. The luminosity of candidate “lines” in the galaxy’s emission sets constraints on such broadcast populations.

VIII.5
The diffuse approximation

Because societies are discrete, they clump broadcasts. However, including the societal level in the tree greatly complicates the analysis, compounding the Poissonian character of the number of broadcasts in collections of societies. The
diffuse approximation
ignores the discreteness of societies, instead imagining the transmitters being spread diffusely across the galaxy. The variance in
{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{B}}}
is thus underestimated. How large this correction is depends on the nature of the discretization, but the diffuse approximation gives good results when there are many societies, few of which emit a detectable broadcast (
{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}\ll 1
). This is appropriate when using very fine observations (e.g., narrow channels for narrowband lines) except in the most extreme cases – far, far into the confusion regime for heavily populated galaxies.

Practically speaking, the diffuse approximation treats the broadcasts as the children of metasocieties (expansive metasociety scenario) or galaxies (classical and Galactic club scenario). Thus, the diffuse approximation works directly with the metasociety’s or galaxy’s broadcast distribution instead of building those up from societies’ broadcast distributions. Just as the societal distribution posits that societies trace stars (Section
V.3
&
VI.3
), in the diffuse approximation, that dependence is shifted down to the broadcast distribution. In the end, the broadcast distribution for the effective parent
{{\mathrm{J}}}
in the diffuse approximation is found by substituting

\breve{\mathnormal{\Lambda}}_{\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\updelta(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\mathrm{B}}-\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\mathrm{C}})\to{\mathnormal{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\frac{d\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\star}}\right\rangle}{d\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\star}}(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\mathrm{B}});

(106)

the substitution can also apply to the broadcast abundance per frequency variable.

VIII.6
Aggregate luminosities in diverse, noninterchangeable hosts: an example

Diversity among host objects increases the sampling variance of aggregate quantities, and the variance increases as it manifests in higher-level ancestors. This section presents a simple example: how would the cosmic-relative variance in an aggregate metasocietal luminosity
\mathring{\mathnormal{L}}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}
change if the luminosities were shared among different types of hosts? To keep the example focused, the number distributions
\mathnormal{N}_{\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}{(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{C}})}\sim\mathrm{Pois}(\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle)
and
\mathnormal{N}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}{(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{w}}_{\mathrm{M}})}\sim\mathrm{Pois}(\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right\rangle)
are strictly the same among different societies and metasocieties. In case 1, every metasociety and society is interchangeable, with

\uppsi\left[\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathrm{C}}(\ell)=\exp(-\ell/\overline{\mathnormal{\ell}})/\overline{\mathnormal{\ell}}.

(107)

In case 2, all the broadcasts in an individual society have the same luminosity, but this characteristic luminosity is exponentially distributed between societies:

\uppsi\left[\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathrm{C}}(\ell)=\updelta(\ell-\overline{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{C}})~\text{and}~\uppsi\left[\overline{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{C}}\right]_{\mathrm{M}}(\ell)=\exp(-\ell/\overline{\mathnormal{\ell}})/\overline{\mathnormal{\ell}}.

(108)

In case 3, each metasociety has decreed all broadcasts have the same luminosity, but different metasocieties decide on different luminosities, with an exponential distribution:

\uppsi\left[\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathrm{M}}(\ell)=\updelta(\ell-\overline{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{M}})~\text{and}~\uppsi\left[\overline{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{M}}\right]_{\mathrm{U}}(\ell)=\exp(-\ell/\overline{\mathnormal{\ell}})/\overline{\mathnormal{\ell}}.

(109)

These can be compared with case 0, in which all broadcasts have the same luminosity:
\uppsi\left[\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathrm{C}}(\ell)=\updelta(\ell-\overline{\mathnormal{\ell}})
. In case 0, broadcasts, societies, and metasocieties are all interchangeable.

There are slightly different approaches to calculating the variance in each case, but it involves building up from broadcast-level averages to the cosmic level. All the broadcasts in a society are drawn from the same luminosity distribution – a degenerate one in cases 0, 2, and 3, and an exponential one in case 1. Thus, the aggregate luminosity of broadcasts in a realized society is a compound Poisson variable. In cases 0, 1, and 3, the societies are interchangeable, so we can then apply a compound Poisson distribution again. However, in case 2, the societies have different luminosity distributions depending on their
\overline{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{C}}
. To advance to the metasocietal-level variables, we have to integrate over
\uppsi\left[\overline{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{C}}\right]_{\mathrm{M}}
, which is a sort of marginalized societal haystack (equation
10
). Finally, in cases 0–2, all metasocieties are interchangeable, and so
\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{L}}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle=\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{L}}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{U}}
and
\mathbb{V}\left[\mathring{\mathnormal{L}}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right]=\mathbb{V}\left[\mathring{\mathnormal{L}}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathrm{U}}
. However, in case 3, we need to find
\mathbb{V}\left[\mathring{\mathnormal{L}}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathrm{U}}=\left\langle(\mathring{\mathnormal{L}}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}})^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{U}}-\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{L}}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{U}}^{2}
by averaging over
\uppsi\left[\overline{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{C}}\right]_{\mathrm{U}}
(equation
43
), effectively a metasocietal haystack.

In all four cases, the mean aggregate luminosity is the same,
\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{L}}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{U}}=\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle\overline{\mathnormal{\ell}}
, because the mean number of broadcasts and the mean broadcast luminosity are the same. The variance, however, increases from one case to the next:

\mathbb{V}\left[\mathring{\mathnormal{L}}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathrm{U}}=\begin{cases}2\overline{\mathnormal{\ell}}^{2}\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle)&(\text{Case 0})\\
2\overline{\mathnormal{\ell}}^{2}\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle(1+\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle)&(\text{Case 1})\\
2\overline{\mathnormal{\ell}}^{2}\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle(1+\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle)&(\text{Case 2})\\
2\overline{\mathnormal{\ell}}^{2}\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle(1+\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle)&(\text{Case 3})\end{cases}

(110)

In case 0, the variance is totally due to compound Poissonian fluctuations in the number of broadcasts, supplemented by intrinsic variance in the luminosity in case 1. All the broadcasts in a society share the same luminosity in cases 2 and 3, amplifying the fluctuations; in case 3, as the shared luminosity
\overline{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{M}}
varies, the entire aggregate luminosity does as well.

This example only scratches the surface of the subject, but the methods in sections
II
and
III
can be applied to these problems even when the compound Poisson distribution does not apply.

IX
The box model

In the box model, broadcasts are simple contiguous “boxes” in time-frequency space. Each box covers the frequency range
|\nu-\mathnormal{\upsilon}_{\mathrm{B}}|\leq\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}/2
and time range
0\leq t-\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{B}}\leq\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}
. Because these boxes are not skewed, the drift rate
\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}
is
0
. Furthermore, all the boxes are assumed to have identical bandwidths
\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}
and durations
\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}
(Figure
7
). The distribution is assumed to be stationary, in that
\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{B}}
and
\mathnormal{\upsilon}_{\mathrm{B}}
have uniform distributions over any observable range, with

{\mathnormal{{\Psi}}_{\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}}={\breve{\mathnormal{\Lambda}}_{\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\uppsi\left[\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\varpi}_{\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathrm{C}}\updelta(\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{B}}-\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}})\updelta(\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{t}|\mathrm{B}}-\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}})\updelta(\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}){\updelta(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\mathrm{B}}-\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\mathrm{C}}).}

(111)

In the box model,
\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{x}{:}t|\mathrm{B}}=\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}
(equations
85
and
98
).

The selection itself is also a contiguous box in time-frequency space, spanning the frequency range
|\nu-\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}|\leq\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}/2
and time range
0\leq t-\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{x}}\leq\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}
. Sample properties in the box model depend on the number and amount of overlap between broadcast boxes and the sample box in time-frequency space (Figure
7
).

Consider a sample
{\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}
, consisting of broadcasts within a field of
{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\star}}
stars with a time and frequency response described by a box. The number of broadcasts expected from a host
{{\mathrm{J}}}
is proportional to the rate per star per unit frequency, number of stars, and the temporal and frequency “cross sections” resulting from the combined width of the window and the broadcasts:

\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle={\mathnormal{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\star}\right\rangle}(\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}+\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}})(\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}+\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}).

(112)

Figure 7:
Sketch of a spectrogram in the box model. All broadcasts have equal duration
\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}
and bandwidth
\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}
, and
\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\nu;\mathrm{B}}
is constant within each “box” although the total
\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}
may vary (filled boxes of different shades). The selection also is a window of duration
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}
and bandwidth observation
\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}
at all times and frequencies within those ranges. Only emission within the selection (blue shading) is observed.

Observables in the box model tie to an overlap quantity
\mathnormal{o}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}
, the fraction of the broadcast’s time-frequency “area” within the sample box. Appendix
B
provides calculations relating to this quantity.

As uniform “boxes” in time-frequency space, broadcasts in the box model have steady luminosities and flat spectra:

\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\nu\mathchar 59\relax p;\mathrm{B}}(t\mathchar 59\relax\nu\mathchar 59\relax p)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{p}|\mathrm{B}}(p)\frac{\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}}{\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}}&\begin{array}[]{l}\text{if}~0\leq t-\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{B}}\leq\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}\\
\text{and}~|\nu-\mathnormal{\upsilon}_{\mathrm{B}}|\leq\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}/2\end{array}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}.

(113)

Although the broadcasts may share the same degree and type of polarization, the polarization state is random. The mean luminosity spectrum of a sample is

\left\langle{\mathring{\mathnormal{L}}_{\nu\mathchar 59\relax p;\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}(t\mathchar 59\relax\nu\mathchar 59\relax p)\right\rangle=\frac{1}{2}{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\star}\right\rangle}{\mathnormal{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}}.

(114)

with a mean energy fluence of

\left\langle{\mathfrak{H}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle=\frac{|\mathsf{\Pi}_{\mathsf{x}}|}{2}{\mathnormal{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\star}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}\left\langle{y}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathrm{B}}{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}}\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}.

(115)

Many observational quantities simplify when we assume that
\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}\nsim\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}
and
\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}\nsim\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}
. The box model has four natural limits:

•

Lines
– Long-lasting, narrowband broadcasts with
\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}\gg\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}
but
\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}\ll\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}
. These include the classic carrier wave “beacons” of radio SETI (
Drake 49
; see also
Enriquez et al. 56
and references therein) and artificial laser lines in optical
[
157
]
. Instead of
\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}
and
{\mathnormal{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}
, it is most natural to consider the isotropic luminosity
\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}\equiv\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}/\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}
. As the lifetime is unknown and largely irrelevant, the observable rate is
{\mathnormal{Z}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}={\mathnormal{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}{\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}={\mathnormal{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}
.

•

Pulses
– Short-lasting, wideband broadcasts with
\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}\ll\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}
and
\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}\gg\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}
. Several SETI surveys have sought pulses (e.g.,
Shvartsman et al. 163
;
Howard et al. 89
;
Siemion et al. 164
,
Maire et al. 126
). Instead of
\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}
, it is most natural to consider the isotropic energy spectrum
\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\nu;\mathrm{B}}\equiv\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}/\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}
. The observable rate is
{\mathnormal{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}={\mathnormal{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}{\left\langle\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}={\mathnormal{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}
.

•

Blips
– Short-lasting, narrowband broadcasts with
\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}\ll\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}
and
\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}\ll\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}
. Blips can form the basis of wideband communication systems
[
129
]
, or result from transient broadcasts like our radars or narrowbeam beacons on rotating worlds
[
69
]
.

•

Hisses
– Long-lasting, wideband broadcasts with
\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}\gg\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}
and
\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}\gg\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}
, essentially noise. Thermal waste heat
[
53
]
is effectively a hiss, as is exhaust radiation
[
81
]
, and a typical random waveform is a hiss similar to white noise. Long-lasting continuum sources emit hisses. Instead of
\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}
, it is most natural to consider the isotropic luminosity spectrum
\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\nu;\mathrm{B}}\equiv\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}/(\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}})
. The observable abundance is
{\mathnormal{\Xi}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}={\mathnormal{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}{\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}\left\langle\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}={\mathnormal{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}
.

Table
10
summarizes key quantities for each of these regimes.
