{\mathnormal{Z}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}
,
{\mathnormal{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}
,
{\mathnormal{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}
,
{\mathnormal{\Xi}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}
), window definition quantities (
\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}
,
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}
), broadcast durations and bandwidths (
\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}
,
\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}
), broadcast emission (
\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}
,
\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\nu;\mathrm{B}}
,
\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}
,
\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\nu;\mathrm{B}}
,
\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}
,
{\mathring{\mathnormal{L}}_{\nu;\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}
).

Quantities in observer frame: fluence (
\mathfrak{h}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}
).

When considering a fixed sample of stars, the known number of stars
\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\star}
can be substituted for
\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\star}\right\rangle
.

Often there are multiple time and frequency scales involved in a survey. A broadcast might be longer than a few seconds long exposure but multiple pointings of the same sky region could occur over years. The approximations for the four quadrants apply when the inequalities hold for all relevant time and frequency scales.

X
The chord model for frequency-drifting lines

The broadcasts in the box model have no skew. Although a satisfactory approximation when the time or frequency resolution is coarse, both line and pulse searches use fine sampling windows sensitive to frequency drifts – lines drift because of the changing Doppler shifts of an accelerating source
[
159
]
, while pulses drift because of dispersion induced by the interstellar and intergalactic media
[
164
]
. In fact, lines without any drift or dispersion are generally attributable to anthropogenic radio frequency interference, since the rotation of the Earth will add a location-dependent frequency drift
[
56
,
160
]
. Individual broadcasts can be dedrifted or dedispersed by shifting the spectrum by different delays in different channels to maximize signal-to-noise (see Paper II). Nonetheless, a population of broadcasts form a background with a range of drift rates that cannot be dedrifted.

An archetypal drifting line never “ends” in the middle of an observational sample window, because
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}\ll\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{B}}
. To avoid edge effects, these broadcasts can be considered as thin bands with an instantaneous bandwidth
\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{t}|\mathrm{B}}
and a duration at fixed frequency
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\upnu|\mathrm{B}}
(Figure
8
). The ratio of these quantities defines the slope or drift rate

|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|\equiv\frac{\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{t}|\mathrm{B}}}{\mathnormal{\tau}_{\upnu|\mathrm{B}}}.

(116)

The drift rate is a signed quantity that is positive if the broadcast drifts to high frequencies at later times and negative if it drifts to low frequencies. In the chord model,
\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}
does not vary with time – broadcasts have linear drift in frequency. As in the box model, the selection window is a contiguous box in time-frequency space.

The chord model simplifies further by taking these bands to have negligible
\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{t}|\mathrm{B}}
and
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\upnu|\mathrm{B}}
, even as
\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}
itself is fixed. Because the band has infinitesimal bandwidth, the broadcast is either entirely within the sample or not at any given time (gray-blue line in Figure
8
). A broadcast is part of the sample if the band ever crosses the box, appearing as a chord in time-frequency diagrams. The duration of the chord’s time in the sample box is
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}
, and it sets the amount of emission received. The effective bandwidth used in calculating the expected number of broadcasts in the sample is
\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{x}{:}t|\mathrm{B}}=|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{B}}
(equations
98
and
85
). The chord approximation breaks down if
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\upnu|\mathrm{B}}\gtrsim\min(\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}/|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|)
.

Figure 8:
Sketch of a spectrogram in the chord model. Drifting signals could resemble a band (red) on the diagram. In the chord approximation, the band is assumed to have negligible bandwidth and looks like a skewed line (gray). A nonzero fluence is intercepted by the window only if the line cuts across the window box in a chord (highlighted in blue).

I adopt a distribution for drifting broadcasts similar to that for lines in the box model, with a frequency abundance of
\breve{\mathnormal{Z}}_{\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}
per society:

{\mathnormal{{\Psi}}_{\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}}={\breve{\mathnormal{Z}}_{\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\updelta(\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{B}}-\mathnormal{\tau}_{\infty})\updelta(\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{t}|\mathrm{B}})\updelta(\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}){\updelta(\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\mathrm{B}}-\boldsymbol{\mathnormal{r}}_{\mathrm{C}})}{\updelta(\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{B}})}\\
{\cdot~\uppsi\left[\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\varpi}_{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathrm{C}}.}

(117)

The origin time of each broadcast is set to
t=0
while the duration is set to an arbitrarily long
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\infty}
; the distribution is then assumed to extend to all negative and positive frequencies with the understanding that emission at frequencies and times outside any window being used is ignored. This allows for a direct relation between
\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}
and the more relevant effective isotropic luminosity
\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}=\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}/\mathnormal{\tau}_{\infty}
, with
\uppsi\left[\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathrm{C}}=\mathnormal{\tau}_{\infty}\uppsi\left[\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathrm{C}}
. The mean number of broadcasts within
{\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}
is

\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle={\mathnormal{Z}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\star}\right\rangle}(\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}+\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}{\left\langle|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}})\mathchar 59\relax

(118)

with an average time spent within
{\mathsf{x}}
of

{\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}=\frac{\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}}{1+\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}{\left\langle|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}/\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}}.

(119)

The drift rate distribution is not evenly sampled. Broadcasts with high
{|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|}
are more likely to cut through the box simply because they cover more frequency, although they spend less time on average within the observational window. Calculations of statistical quantities need to take this into account (see Appendix
C
, equation
C9
). Although the biasing can be extreme for individual observations in single channels, modern radio SETI surveys cover hundreds of MHz or more and thus should sample drift rates more or less fairly.

A drift rate distribution is necessary to calculate the higher moments. In some cases, all
\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}
may have the same value, if all broadcasts are coming from transmitters at a single location within a narrow frequency range. A galactic population of line transmitters may contain a panoply of sites with different accelerations with a potentially vast
\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}
range
[
159
]
. In this series, I adopt a uniform drift rate distribution:

{\uppsi\left[\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathrm{J}}}=\mathbb{I}[|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}-{\mathnormal{\Delta}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}|\leq{\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{J}}}]/(2{\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{J}}})

(120)

where
{\mathnormal{\Delta}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\equiv{\left\langle\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}
is the “center” of the distribution, which may be nonzero because of Doppler effects imposed by the Earth’s rotation and revolution. A canonical value for the drift rate scale is
{\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{J}}}=1\ \textrm{Hz}\,\sec^{-1}(\nu/\textrm{GHz})

[
140
]
, though there could be a tail extending to much higher
\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}

[
159
]
. Note this is the unbiased drift rate distribution.

The energy emission in this model is treated as being defined by a steady luminosity:

\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\nu\mathchar 59\relax p;\mathrm{B}}(t\mathchar 59\relax\nu\mathchar 59\relax p)=\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{p}|\mathrm{B}}(p)\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}\updelta(\nu-\mathnormal{\upsilon}_{\mathsf{t}|\mathrm{B}}(t))\mathchar 59\relax

(121)

where
\mathnormal{\upsilon}_{\mathsf{t}|\mathrm{B}}(t)
is the frequency of the chord at time
t
. Taking the limit of broadcasts with small
\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{t}|\mathrm{B}}
allows us to calculate

\left\langle{\mathring{\mathnormal{L}}_{\nu;\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}(t\mathchar 59\relax\nu)\right\rangle=\frac{|\mathsf{\Pi}_{\mathsf{x}}|}{2}{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\star}\right\rangle}{\mathnormal{Z}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}}.

(122)

This is a smooth function although any realized
{\mathring{\mathnormal{L}}_{\nu;\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}
itself is very “spiky.” Windows integrate over these “spikes” with their nonzero bandwidth, yielding a mean fluence of

\left\langle{\mathfrak{H}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle=\frac{|\mathsf{\Pi}_{\mathsf{x}}|}{2}{\mathnormal{Z}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\star}\right\rangle}{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}}{\left\langle{y}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathrm{B}}{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}}\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}.

(123)

Further details and formulae are given in Appendix
C
.

XI
Measurements and noise

The final step from populations to observables is the measurement itself. A full discussion of measurements and instrumental effects is deferred (see Paper II), but it is worth noting that measurements are random variables that depend on the sample. They generally depend on a detector response
\mathcal{I}
that varies with position, but this distribution is predictable for each point on the haystack, and the observables are tractable with the point process framework. An archetypal observable is an aggregate variable summing a quantity
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}
(with
\left\langle\mathfrak{m}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle\propto\mathfrak{u}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}
) for every broadcast in a sample mixed with some kind of background. Measurements can also include derived quantities that describe the statistics of integrated observables. A simple example is the derived signal-to-noise ratio, which is discussed in detail in Paper II. Others include cross-correlation statistics to detect large populations of faint signals
[
50
]
. Another major type is counts of objects fulfilling a particular criterion.

Actual measurements are subject to noise, microscopic fluctuations in the instrument, background radiation, or the broadcast radiation itself. The noise increases the variance in observables beyond the sampling variance, making individual broadcasts harder to detect. If we are doing a measurement on a broadcast sample
\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\mathrm{B}}
, the variance in that measurement is (equation
2
)

\mathbb{V}\left[\mathfrak{m}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\right]=\left\langle\mathbb{V}\left[\mathfrak{m}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}|\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right]\right\rangle+\mathbb{V}\left[\left\langle\mathfrak{m}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}|\mathnormal{\Sigma}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle\right]\mathchar 59\relax

(124)

the first term being the mean noise variance and the second representing sample variance.

XII
Where should we look for extragalactic broadcasts?

The idea that starfaring societies replicate until they pervade a galaxy suggests large galaxies as disproportionately favorable targets for SETI.

It is plausible that spreading metasocieties are very rare, with
\ll 1
on average per Milky Way-sized galaxy. The lack of evidence for an omnipresent metasociety in our own Galaxy is suggestive, although perhaps that is the result of anthropic selection effects
[
78
]
or active measures to remain hidden
[
13
]
. Rarity is also consistent with the lack of evidence for Kardashev Type III metasocieties
[
4
,
63
,
71
,
108
,
27
]
and negative extragalactic SETI results (
Horowitz & Sagan 88
,
Shostak et al. 162
,
Gray & Mooley 70
). With the framework developed, we now consider how many broadcasts to expect from galaxies of differing sizes, contrasting galactic clubs and spreading metasocieties.

Figure 9:
Number density of galaxies with at least one detected beacon in galactic clubs (dashed,
\mathnormal{\Xi}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{C}}=0.1
) and expansive metasocieties (solid for
\mathnormal{\Xi}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}=0}=10^{-12}
and dotted for
\mathnormal{\Xi}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}=0}=1
, both with
\mathnormal{\Xi}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}=1
). Different colors are used for different
\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle
:
100
(black),
10^{-10}
(red),
10^{-12}
(gold), and
10^{-14}
(blue). The fraction of galaxies with detectable beacons can be near
1
(common broadcasts and societies), increase linearly with stellar mass (galactic clubs or common expansive metasocieties), or increase quadratically with stellar mass (rare expansive metasocieties.)

Suppose, under the interchangeability and independence assumptions, that societies in populated galaxies create ultranarrowband beacons, all very bright and easily detected out to cosmological distances. In the line regime of the box model (or equivalently, the chord model with
{\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{G}}}=0
),
\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle=\breve{\mathnormal{Z}}_{\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{s}}
for survey
{\mathsf{s}}
.

What is the probability that
{\mathsf{s}}
intercepts at least one such broadcast in a comoving volume? And do we expect them in the more numerous small galaxies, or in the rarer large galaxies? If these beacons are sufficiently rare, most observed societies have none active within the band observed by the survey (
\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{C}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle\ll 1
), meaning equation
102
is a good estimate of the probability that at least one broadcast passing the luminosity cut is intercepted. The stellar mass density distribution of galaxies with a detectable broadcast is given by

\frac{d^{2}\left\langle\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{G}}(\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{B}}\geq 1)\right\rangle}{dVd\ln{\left\langle\mathnormal{M}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\star}\right\rangle}}=\mathnormal{P}(\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{B}}\geq 1)\frac{d^{2}\left\langle\mathnormal{N}^{\mathrm{G}}\right\rangle}{dVd\ln{\left\langle\mathnormal{M}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\star}\right\rangle}}.

(125)

To find this mass function of galaxies with detected beacons, I adopt the
z\sim 0.1
galaxy mass distributions from
Moustakas et al. [131]
, and assume
\left\langle\mathnormal{m}_{\star}\right\rangle=0.2\ M_{\odot}
motivated by
Chabrier [26]
.

I compare the expansive metasociety and galactic club scenario in Figure
9
. Expansive metasocieties are assumed by default to be fairly rare (
\mathnormal{\Xi}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}=0}=10^{-12}
) but they plant one society around every star (
\mathnormal{\Xi}_{\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}=1
), so that
\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right\rangle\approx 10^{10}
for
\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\star}\right\rangle=\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\star}\right\rangle=10^{11}
, a moderate sized galaxy. The comparable galactic club scenario has
\mathnormal{\Xi}_{\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{C}}=0.1
, so that the mean number of societies in a galaxy of the same size is nearly equal,
\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{C}}\right\rangle=10^{10}
for
\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\star}\right\rangle=10^{11}
. This is likely extremely generous to the galactic club models because abundances this high require a mean societal lifespan of order
1\ \textrm{Gyr}
. Note that in the galactic club scenario, the societies are basically spread evenly between galaxies, while in the expansive metasociety scenario, only about
10\%
of
\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\star}\right\rangle=10^{11}
galaxies are inhabited, but these have
10
times the mean number of societies.

As seen in Figure
9
, there are two regimes of behavior: an asymptotic density distribution when
\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle\gg\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\star}\right\rangle^{-1}
, and a steeper form when
\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle\ll\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\star}\right\rangle^{-1}
. In the latter case, broadcasts are rare, and thus bigger galaxies are more likely to have one. Detections are biased to more massive galaxies in the expansive metasociety scenario, where
\mathnormal{P}({\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\geq 1)
has a quadratic dependence on the expected number of stars at the present,
\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\star}\right\rangle
.

The reason for the quadratic dependence is simple: both
\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{M}}\right\rangle
and
\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{s}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{M}}^{\mathrm{C}}}\right\rangle
independently grow with stellar mass. When expansive metasocieties are rare, larger galaxies are proportionately more likely to host one simply because they have more stars for them to evolve around. Then, when one does appear and spread across the galaxy, there are more stars to populate, resulting in more societies, and thus more broadcasts. In contrast, while more stars mean more societies in the galactic club scenario, each society is confined to one star regardless of galaxy mass.
40
40

40

There are additional reasons to favor larger galaxies: their stellar populations tend to be older
[
62
,
36
, e.g.,]
, allowing for more time for ETIs to evolve, and they tend to have higher metallicities according to the mass-metallicity relationship, suggesting more planets may be around
[
45
]
.
This effect applies only when expansive metasocieties are rare; when they are extremely common (dotted lines in the figure), the linear dependence on mass is restored – though with a higher constant of proportionality, because we expect many more societies in an expansive metasociety.

This demonstration suggests that if interstellar migration is a credible possibility, extragalactic SETI could do well to focus on high-mass galaxies. Breakthrough Listen’s nearby galaxy survey observes several large galaxies, including ellipticals in the Virgo cluster
[
93
]
. It also has commensal access to MeerKAT, which will conduct deep observations of the Fornax cluster, which also is home to many large galaxies
[
41
]
.

XIII
Conclusion

If interstellar travel and migration are indeed possible, then ETIs are unlike known astrophysical phenomena in that they can reproduce. Replication can amplify quirks of history onto galactic scales. Thus, supposing that starfaring ETIs are rare, one galaxy could have no ETIs while another, astrophysically indistinguishable, could have billions of inhabited worlds. This motivates the use of a probabilistic treatment of the observable technosignatures of galaxies, wherein different galaxies can have wildly different broadcast distributions.

This work introduces a framework that walks step by step from this population to measurements. Galaxies are treated as isolated systems within the universe. Within them, ETIs are congregated into localized societies. Entire lineages of societies are aggregated into metasocieties, which can cover the entire galaxy. Broadcasts are produced by societies. These objects are organized in a random tree structure, where objects can have ancestor hosts and in turn host descendants.

Each of these objects is described by a parameter tuple, points in their respective “haystacks”
[
188
, c.f.,]
. Their populations are random point processes in the haystacks, one process defined for every possible host. Every point process has a distribution (intensity), describing the mean number of objects in different parts of the haystack. A selection is a thinning according to position in the haystack, within some window, for some host object. The result is a biased random set, which is realized as a sample. The Poisson point process is a simple description of populations in realized hosts when objects are independent and interchangeable. Observables can then be calculated using compound Poisson distributions. Given a distribution of random objects, the population is described by a mixture of Poisson point processes called a Cox point process. The box model and the chord models specifically describe the observables of broadcasts.

I present several examples and models to show the scope of the framework:

•

Lifespan bias, in which longer-lived objects have a larger temporal “cross section,” is viewed as the time-limited window reaching farther into the haystack as lifespan increases (section
III.8
; Figure
6
). When sampling with a window
{\mathsf{x}}
with duration
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}
, the duration mean is biased as

\left\langle f(\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}})\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{K}}=\frac{{\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}}f(\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{K}}}+\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}{\left\langle f(\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}})\right\rangle_{\mathrm{K}}}}{{\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{K}}}+\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

with each biased moment of object lifespan
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{J}}
for short windows depending on the next unbiased moment. Selection bias also shows up in the chord model, where broadcasts with high drift rates are more likely to be sampled.

•

The formalism can be used to rederive the Drake equation for classical assumptions about the lack of significant interstellar travel (section
VI.4
). It follows from integrating the societal and metasocietal distribution functions in the classical scenario. This work’s version says that the instantaneous mean number of communicative societies in a galaxy is

\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{t}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{G}}^{\mathrm{C}}(t)\right\rangle\approx\breve{\mathnormal{\Gamma}}^{\star}_{\mathrm{G}}\left\langle\mathnormal{f}_{\star}^{\mathrm{M}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}\left\langle\mathnormal{f}^{\mathrm{C}}_{\mathrm{M}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{M}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{G}}\mathord{\mathchar 59\relax}

the product of a constant star formation rate, the mean fraction of stars hosting ETIs, the mean fraction of societies becoming communicative, and the mean lifespan of societies.

•

Expressions for the mean and variance of aggregate random variables are presented for “galactic club” and “expansive metasociety” scenarios are presented (Table
9
), under the interchangeability assumption. They apply to the total intercepted emission from all broadcasts in a galaxy. The fluence of the population is proportional to the number of stars, rate/abundance of broadcasts, mean fluence of individual broadcasts, and additional factors related to the bandwidth and duration of the broadcasts and windows (section
VIII.4
):

\left\langle\mathfrak{U}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle\approx\mathnormal{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\star}\right\rangle(\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}+\left\langle\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{x}{:}t|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}})(\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}+\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}})\\
\cdot\left\langle\mathfrak{u}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}.

(126)

Variance in the aggregate variable increases as the interchangeability assumption is relaxed (Section
VIII.6
).

•

The presence of expansive metasocieties can actually result in the number of detected broadcasts depending quadratically on stellar mass (section
XII
). This is because the number of opportunities for an interstellar metasociety to arise in the first place is proportional to the number of stars, and then independently, the number of sites for independent broadcasting societies is again proportional to the number of stars. Larger galaxies may then be disproportionately likely to host detectable technosignatures (Figure
9
).

Future papers of the series will consider limits on broadcast populations from total radio emission and individual searches in face of confusion (Paper II), as well as constraints on populations of broadcasting galaxies from source counts and commensal searches of background galaxies (Paper III).

The ideas behind the framework can be applied to other phenomena, though with a different hierarchy of objects. Transients like fast radio bursts are generated by discrete objects like neutron stars, each of which could yield a whole population of events. In turn, some kinds of sites may be found only in globular clusters or other subgalactic environments
[
105
, e.g.,]
. Thus, like broadcasts, natural transients can be clustered into multiple levels of hosts, which may be described by point processes.

I thank the Breakthrough Listen program for their support. Funding for
Breakthrough Listen
research is sponsored by the Breakthrough Prize Foundation (
https://breakthroughprize.org/
). In addition, I acknowledge the use of NASA’s Astrophysics Data System and arXiv for this research. I am grateful to the referee for their dedicated work in reviewing Papers I and II, including thorough readings; and I thank the statistics editor for guiding me to useful references.

Appendix A
Extreme value theory and regularization

A.1
Review of basic extreme value theory

Suppose we have a collection of
n
random variables
X_{i}
(with
i
ranging from
1
to
n
), all i.i.d.. We can sort the values assigned to the
X_{i}
and define the order statistic
X_{(i)}
as the
i
th smallest value. The order statistics themselves are random variables, each with their own well-defined probability distribution (e.g.,
Gumbel 73
;
Coles et al. 35
,
Castillo et al. 25
,
Embrechts et al. 55
). Most noteworthy are the minimum
X_{(1)}=\min X_{i}
and the maximum
X_{(n)}=\max X_{i}
. Their probability distributions are easy to calculate, since it is just the probability that every
X_{i}
independently is greater or smaller than
x
. Given the CDF
F[X]
and the complementary cumulative distribution function (CCDF)
\overline{F}[X]\equiv 1-F[X]
, the maximum’s CDF is

{F[X_{(n)}|n](x)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}F[X_{i}](x)=[F[X_{i}](x)]^{n}}

(A1)

and the minimum’s CCDF is

{\overline{F}[X_{(1)}](x)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\overline{F}[X_{i}](x)=[\overline{F}[X_{i}](x)]^{n}}.

(A2)

These random variables generally converge to one of the three types of extreme value distributions – the Gumbel distribution if
X_{i}
has an exponential-like PDF tail (including the normal distribution), the Fréchet distribution when
X_{i}
has a power-law-like PDF tail, and the Weibull distribution when they have finite support
[
73
,
25
, e.g.,]
.

However, when observing a population of astronomical objects in a field, the actual number sampled is a random variable
N
. For Poissonian
N
,
X_{(1)}=\min_{1\leq i\leq N}X_{i}
and
X_{(N)}=\max_{1\leq i\leq N}X_{i}
each have a mixture probability distribution, a weighted sum of the probability distributions conditionalized on each value of
n
:

\displaystyle F[X_{(N)}](x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathnormal{P}(N=n)F[X_{(N)}|N](x|n)}{1-\mathnormal{P}(N=0)}=\displaystyle\frac{e^{\left\langle N\right\rangle F[X](x)}-1}{e^{\left\langle N\right\rangle}-1}

\displaystyle\overline{F}[X_{(1)}](x)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathnormal{P}(N=n)\overline{F}[X_{(1)}|N](x|n)}{1-\mathnormal{P}(N=0)}=\displaystyle\frac{e^{\left\langle N\right\rangle\overline{F}[X](x)}-1}{e^{\left\langle N\right\rangle}-1}

(A3)

[
25
]
. Note that case where
N=0
is specifically excluded because the minimum and maximum
X_{i}
in a sample with zero members is not defined. This exclusion is necessary to ensure the minimum and maximum exist.

A.2
Regularization using median values for minimum and maximum values in a Poissonian sample

In this series, regularization is accomplished by introducing cutoffs in the values of
X
. I use quartiles for the minimum and maximum
X
in a sample, with a lower bound given by
\overline{F}[X_{(1)}](X^{L})=3/4
and an upper bound of
F[X_{(N)}](X^{H})=3/4
. Equation
A3
gives us

\displaystyle F[X](X^{H})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\left\langle N\right\rangle}\ln\left[\frac{3}{4}e^{\left\langle N\right\rangle}+\frac{1}{4}\right]

\displaystyle F[X](X^{L})

\displaystyle=1-\frac{1}{\left\langle N\right\rangle}\ln\left[\frac{3}{4}e^{\left\langle N\right\rangle}+\frac{1}{4}\right].

(A4)

When
\left\langle N\right\rangle\gg 1
, the great majority of the probability mass is sampled. As long as we have the inverse CDF of
X
, the bounds are approximated as
X^{H}\approx F[X]^{-1}(1+\ln(3/4)/\left\langle N\right\rangle)
and
X^{L}\approx F[X]^{-1}(-\ln(3/4)/\left\langle N\right\rangle)
.

When
0<\left\langle N\right\rangle\ll 1
,
F[X](X^{L})
and
F[X](X^{H})
converge to
1/4
and
3/4
, respectively. Roughly, the spread among all
X_{i}
should be the spread in a single
X_{i}
, because virtually all observations have no events and almost all of the rest have
N=1
. We could also use other probability thresholds for
X^{L}
and
X^{H}
. Using the median
X_{(1)}
and
X_{(N)}
is an intuitive choice when
\left\langle N\right\rangle\gtrsim 1
, but for
\left\langle N\right\rangle\ll 1
, it would imply that the regularized variable has a variance approaching zero, and is thus unsatisfactory.

Appendix B
Further details for the box model

The distributions and averages I present here are calculated assuming a uniform spread in time and frequency (uniform
{\mathnormal{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}
) and that
(\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}+\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}})/2\leq\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}
.

B.1
The overlap fraction

If we regard time and frequency as a plane, the overlap fraction
\mathnormal{o}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}
is the area of intersection between two contiguous boxes, scaled to the area of the broadcast’s box:

\mathnormal{o}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\equiv\frac{\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}}{\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}}=\frac{\mathrm{R}[\min(\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{B}}+\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{x}}+\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}})-\max(\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathrm{B}}\mathchar 59\relax\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{x}})]}{\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}}\cdot~\frac{\mathrm{R}\left[\min\left(\mathnormal{\upsilon}_{\mathrm{B}}+\frac{\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}}{2}\mathchar 59\relax\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}+\frac{\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}}{2}\right)-\max\left(\mathnormal{\upsilon}_{\mathrm{B}}-\frac{\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}}{2}\mathchar 59\relax\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}-\frac{\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}}{2}\right)\right]}{\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}}\mathchar 59\relax

(B1)

using the ramp function
\mathrm{R}(x)=\max(0\mathchar 59\relax x)
. The overlap can be decomposed into the intersection between the time ranges spanned by the sample window and the broadcast and the intersection of the frequency ranges spanned by the sample and broadcast. It reaches a maximum value when one of the time ranges is fully inside the other and likewise one frequency range contains the other, although it is possible for the sample window to contain the broadcast for the one and the broadcast to contain the window for the other. This maximal value is

\mathnormal{o}^{\mathrm{max}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}=\min\left(1\mathchar 59\relax\frac{\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}}{\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}}\right)\min\left(1\mathchar 59\relax\frac{\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}}{\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}}\right).

(B2)

Among broadcasts within the sample (with a nonzero
\mathnormal{o}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}
), the probability this value is attained is

\mathnormal{P}(\mathnormal{o}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}=\mathnormal{o}^{\mathrm{max}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}})=\frac{|\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}-\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}||\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}-\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}|}{(\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}+\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}})(\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}+\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}})}.

(B3)

This probability only diverges significantly from
1
if
\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}\approx\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}
or
\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}\approx\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}
.
A priori
we may expect that ETIs and our own efforts are uncoordinated and thus this is unlikely to happen, but there are natural lower limits to bandwidth set by interstellar scattering
[
39
]
, and thus our observations may try to match the broadened bandwidth of a line. In any case, only in rare cases will the partial overlap result in
0<\mathnormal{o}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\ll\mathnormal{o}^{\mathrm{max}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}
. The probability density for this regime is

\uppsi\left[\mathnormal{o}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathsf{x}}(\mathnormal{o}|0<\mathnormal{o}<\mathnormal{o}^{\mathrm{max}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}})=\frac{2}{(\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}+\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}})(\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}+\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}})}\left[\max\left(\frac{\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}}{\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}}\mathchar 59\relax\frac{\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}}{\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}}\right)+\max\left(\frac{\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}}{\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}}\mathchar 59\relax\frac{\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}}{\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}}\right)-2-2\ln\left(\frac{\mathnormal{o}}{\mathnormal{o}^{\mathrm{max}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}}\right)\right]

(B4)

The means used in the next section can be calculated from equations
B2
–
B4
.

B.2
Emission and selection windows

The emission that falls into the selection box is equal to the total emission ever released in the broadcast multiplied by the overlap fraction and the fraction in observed polarizations:
\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}=\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}\mathnormal{o}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}
. Thus the mean restricted energy is

{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}=\frac{|\mathsf{\Pi}_{\mathsf{x}}|}{2}{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}}\frac{\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}}{(\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}+\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}})(\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}+\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}})}.

(B5)

The variance is found using

{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}={\left\langle\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}}\frac{\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}}{(\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}+\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}})(\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}+\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}})}\max\left[1-\frac{\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}}{3\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}}\mathchar 59\relax\frac{\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}}{\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}}-\frac{\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}^{2}}{3\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}^{2}}\right]\max\left[1-\frac{\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}}{3\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}}\mathchar 59\relax\frac{\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}}{\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}}-\frac{\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}^{2}}{3\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}^{2}}\right].

(B6)

Equation
113
implies the number of photons per unit frequency falls as
\nu^{-1}
. I find

{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{q}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}=\frac{|\mathsf{\Pi}_{\mathsf{x}}|}{2}\frac{{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}}}{h}\frac{\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}}{(\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}+\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}})(\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}+\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}})}\ln\left(\frac{1+\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}/(2\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}})}{1-\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}/(2\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}})}\right)\mathchar 59\relax

(B7)

approaching
\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle/(h\mathsf{\Upsilon})
when
\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}\ll\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}
. Furthermore,

{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{q}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}={\left\langle\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}\frac{{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}}}{h}\frac{\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}}{(\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}+\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}})(\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}+\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}})}\max\left[1-\frac{\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}}{3\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}}\mathchar 59\relax\frac{\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}}{\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}}-\frac{\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}^{2}}{3\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}^{2}}\right]\frac{1}{\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}^{2}}\\
{\cdot~\begin{cases}\displaystyle\left[4\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}+4\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}\ln\frac{1-\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}/(2\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}})}{1+\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}/(2\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}})}+\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}\left[\ln\frac{1-\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}/(2\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}})}{1+\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}/(2\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}})}\right]^{2}\right]&\text{if}~\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}\geq\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}\\
\begin{aligned} 2\left[\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}\left(2+\Li_{2}\frac{\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}}{\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}-\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}/2+\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}}-\Li_{2}\frac{\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}}{\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}+\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}/2}-\frac{1}{2}\left[\ln\left(1+\frac{\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}}{\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}-\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}/2}\right)\right]^{2}\right)\right.\\
+\left(\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}-\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}/2+\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}\right)\ln\left(1+\frac{\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}}{\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}-\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}/2}\right)\left[\ln\left(1+\frac{\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}}{\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}-\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}/2}\right)-1\right]\\
\left.-\left(\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}+\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}/2-\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}\right)\ln\left(1{-}\frac{\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}}{\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}+\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}/2}\right)\left[\ln\left(1{-}\frac{\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}}{\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}+\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}/2}\right)-1\right]\right]\end{aligned}&\text{if}~\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}\leq\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}\end{cases}}

(B8)

with
\Li_{2}(x)
being the dilogarithm (Spence’s function). If
\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}\ll\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}
,
{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{q}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}\approx{{\left\langle\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}}/(h\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}})^{2}
.

Mean fluences may be found by multiplying by the mean of the appropriate transmittance/dilution factor,
{\left\langle{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathrm{B}}^{n}}{y}_{\mathnormal{\varrho};\mathrm{B}}^{n}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}}
for
{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\varrho}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}^{n}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}}
(equation
91
).

Approximations when
\overline{\mathnormal{\tau}}\nsim\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}
and
\overline{\mathnormal{\beta}}\nsim\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}
are given in Table
10
.

Appendix C
Further details for the chord model

The results presented here assume a constant
{\mathnormal{Z}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}
.

C.1
Selection of chords in a contiguous selection window

In the chord model, the window on selected times and frequency is a contiguous “box,” as in the box model. Because we only constrain the behavior of the broadcast within the sample window, calculations are simplified when we “cut” the chord, only considering its behavior at times between
\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{x}}
and
\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{x}}+\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}
. This imposes
\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\mathsf{x}{:}t|\mathrm{B}}=\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{x}}
,
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{x}{:}t|\mathrm{B}}=\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}
, and
\mathnormal{\beta}_{\mathsf{x}{:}t|\mathrm{B}}=|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}
. I also use
\mathnormal{\upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}{:}t|\mathrm{B}}
, the frequency during the mid-time of the window. At other times, the band is centered on a frequency
\mathnormal{\upsilon}_{\mathsf{t}|\mathrm{B}}(t)=\mathnormal{\upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}{:}t|\mathrm{B}}+(t-\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{x}}-\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}/2)\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}
. At any given frequency, the center of the band crosses at time
\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\upnu|\mathrm{B}}(\nu)=(\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{x}}+\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}/2)+(\nu-\mathnormal{\upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}{:}t|\mathrm{B}})/\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}
. Note that
\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}=\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\oplus}/(1+{z})
,
\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}=\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}^{\oplus}(1+{z})
, and
\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}=\mathnormal{\delta}^{\oplus}_{\mathrm{B}}(1+{z})^{2}
are in the source frame.

A broadcast crosses the selection window if
|\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}-\mathnormal{\upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}{:}t|\mathrm{B}}|\leq(\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}+\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|)/2
. The mean number of broadcasts intercepted is thus given by

\frac{d\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle}{d\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}}={\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\star}\right\rangle}{\mathnormal{Z}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}(\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}+\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|){\uppsi\left[\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathrm{J}}}\mathchar 59\relax

(C1)

noting that
{\uppsi\left[\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathrm{J}}}
is the unbiased drift rate distribution. It immediately follows that
\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle={\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\star}\right\rangle}{\mathnormal{Z}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}(\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}+\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}{\left\langle|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}})
, regardless of distribution, where
{\left\langle|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}
is the unbiased mean drift rate magnitude. The biased drift rate distribution, that is, the distribution sampled by the window, favors high drift rate broadcasts:

{\uppsi\left[\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}}{(\mathnormal{\delta})}=\frac{1}{\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle}\frac{d\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle}{d\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}}{(\mathnormal{\delta})}=\frac{\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}+\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}|{\mathnormal{\delta}}|}{\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}+\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}{\left\langle|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}}{\uppsi\left[\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathrm{J}}}{(\mathnormal{\delta})}\mathchar 59\relax

(C2)

(see equation
36
).

When expressing calculations, it is convenient to define dimensionless variables, placing the drift rates in “natural” units defined by the window:
\mathnormal{u}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{B}}\equiv\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}/\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}
and
{\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}={\mathnormal{\Delta}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}/\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}
. Chords with
|\mathnormal{u}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{B}}|\ll 1
behave like lines in the box model.

According to the uniform drift rate distribution model, the drift rates have a uniform unbiased distribution (equation
120
) spanning the range given by
|{\mathnormal{\Delta}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}-\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|\leq{\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{J}}}
. We have

{\left\langle|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}=\begin{cases}{\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{J}}}/2{\cdot}(1+({\mathnormal{\Delta}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}/{\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{J}}})^{2})&{\text{if}~|{\mathnormal{\Delta}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}|\leq{\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{J}}}}\\
|{\mathnormal{\Delta}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}|&{\text{if}~|{\mathnormal{\Delta}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}|\geq{\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{J}}}}\end{cases}

(C3)

and
{\left\langle\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}={\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{J}}}^{2}/3+{{\mathnormal{\Delta}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}}^{2}
. The mean drift rate of a sampled broadcast comes from equation
C2
:

{\left\langle|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{{\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{J}}}}{2}\frac{1+(2/3){\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}+2({\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}})^{2}/{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}+({\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}/{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}})^{2}}{1+(1/2)({\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}+({\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}})^{2}/{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}})}&\text{if}~|{\mathnormal{\Delta}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}|\leq{\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{J}}}\\
\displaystyle\frac{\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}}{\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}}\frac{{(1/3)}{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}^{2}+|{\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}|+({\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}})^{2}}{1+|{\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}|}&\text{if}~|{\mathnormal{\Delta}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}|~{\geq}{\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{J}}}\end{cases}\mathchar 59\relax

(C4)

with
{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}\equiv{\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{J}}}\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}/\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}
.

Furthermore, although the unbiased drift rate magnitude CDF is
F\left[|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|\right]_{\mathrm{J}}(\mathnormal{\delta})=F\left[|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|\right]_{\mathrm{J}}(\mathnormal{\delta})-F\left[|\mathnormal{\delta}|\right]_{\mathrm{J}}(-\mathnormal{\delta})
, the biased CDF is

{F\left[|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|\right]_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}}({\mathnormal{u}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{B}}})=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{{\mathnormal{u}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{B}}}}{{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}}\frac{1+(1/2){\mathnormal{u}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{B}}}}{1+(1/2)({\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}+({\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}})^{2}/{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}})}&\text{if}~|{\mathnormal{\Delta}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}|\leq{\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{J}}}~\text{and}~{\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{J}}}-|{\mathnormal{\Delta}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}|\geq\mathnormal{\delta}\\
\displaystyle\frac{{\mathnormal{u}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{B}}}^{2}+2{\mathnormal{u}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{B}}}+2({\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}-|{\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}|)+({\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}-|{\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}|)^{2}}{4{\mathnormal{u}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{B}}}[1+(1/2)({\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}+({\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}})^{2}/{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}})]}&\text{if}~|{\mathnormal{\Delta}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}|\leq{\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{J}}}~\text{and}~{\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{J}}}-|{\mathnormal{\Delta}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}|\leq\mathnormal{\delta}\leq{\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{J}}}+|{\mathnormal{\Delta}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}|\\
0&\text{if}~|{\mathnormal{\Delta}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}|\geq{\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{J}}}~\text{and}~|{\mathnormal{\Delta}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}|-{\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{J}}}\geq\mathnormal{\delta}\\
\displaystyle\frac{{\mathnormal{u}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{B}}}^{2}+2{\mathnormal{u}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{B}}}-2(|{\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}|-{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}){{-}}(|{\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}|-{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}})^{2}}{4{\mathnormal{u}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{B}}}[1+|{\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}|]}&\text{if}~|{\mathnormal{\Delta}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}|\geq{\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{J}}}~\text{and}~|{\mathnormal{\Delta}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}|-{\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{J}}}\leq\mathnormal{\delta}\leq|{\mathnormal{\Delta}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}|+{\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{J}}}\\
1&\text{if}~{\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{J}}}+|{\mathnormal{\Delta}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}|\leq\mathnormal{\delta}\end{cases}\mathchar 59\relax

(C5)

with
{\mathnormal{u}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{B}}}\equiv\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}/\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}
. When the drift rates span a large range (
{\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{J}}}\gg\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}/\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}
and
\geq|{\mathnormal{\Delta}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}|
), covering the entire range of integration, the biased probability has a quadratic dependence on
\mathnormal{\delta}/{\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{J}}}
.

C.2
Statistics of chord duration in sample

The central quantity in the chord model is
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}
, the time it takes the chord to cross the window:

\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}=\min[\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\upnu|\mathrm{B}}(\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}+\sign(\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}})\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}/2)\mathchar 59\relax\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{x}}+\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}]-\max[\mathnormal{\vartheta}_{\upnu|\mathrm{B}}(\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}-\sign(\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}})\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}/2)\mathchar 59\relax\mathsf{\Theta}_{\mathsf{x}}]

(C6)

It can be shown that

\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\min\left(\frac{\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}}{|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|}\mathchar 59\relax\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}\right)&\displaystyle\text{if}~|\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}-\mathnormal{\upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}{:}t|\mathrm{B}}|\leq\frac{|\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|-\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}|}{2}\\
\displaystyle\frac{\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}+\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}/|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|}{2}-\frac{|\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}-\mathnormal{\upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}{:}t|\mathrm{B}}|}{|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|}&\displaystyle\text{if}~\frac{|\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|-\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}|}{2}\leq|\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}-\mathnormal{\upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}{:}t|\mathrm{B}}|\leq\frac{|\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|+\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}|}{2}\\
0&\displaystyle\text{if}~|\mathsf{\Upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}}-\mathnormal{\upsilon}_{\mathsf{x}{:}t|\mathrm{B}}|\geq\frac{|\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|+\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}|}{2}.\end{cases}

(C7)

For a population in a host
{{\mathrm{J}}}
with a uniform
\mathnormal{\upsilon}_{\mathrm{B}}
distribution, sampled broadcasts with a fixed drift rate (necessarily with nonzero
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}
) have:

{{\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}^{n}|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}}=\frac{\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}^{n}}{1+|\mathnormal{u}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{B}}|}\begin{cases}\displaystyle 1-\frac{n-1}{n+1}|\mathnormal{u}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{B}}|&\text{if}~|\mathnormal{u}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{B}}|\leq 1~\text{and}~n\neq-1\\
\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathnormal{u}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{B}}|^{n}}\left(|\mathnormal{u}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{B}}|-\frac{n-1}{n+1}\right)&\text{if}~|\mathnormal{u}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{B}}|\geq 1~\text{and}~n\neq-1\end{cases}}

(C8)

The mean of a random variable for a broadcast sample is weighted by the number of broadcasts that are in the sample. This leads to the biasing of the mean toward high drift rate broadcasts:

{\left\langle f(\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}})\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\left\langle f(\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}})|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}}{\uppsi\left[\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}}d\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}=\frac{1}{\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}+\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}{\left\langle|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}+\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|)\left\langle f(\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}})|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle{\uppsi\left[\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathrm{J}}}d\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}.

(C9)

for the random variable defined by applying the function
f
to
\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}
. This immediately gives us the
{\mathsf{x}}
-relative mean

{\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}=\frac{\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}}{1+\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}{\left\langle|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}/\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}}

(C10)

For the uniform drift rate distribution, I find:

{\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}=\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}{\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}\cdot~\\
\begin{cases}\displaystyle 1-(1/6)({\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}+({\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}})^{2}/{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}})&\begin{aligned} \text{if}~&|{\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}|\leq{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}~\text{and}\\
&{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}+|{\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}|\leq 1\end{aligned}\\
\displaystyle\frac{1}{4{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}}\left[1+2({\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}-|{\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}|)-(1/3)({\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}-|{\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}|)^{2}+2\ln({\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}+|{\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}|)+\frac{(2/3)}{{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}+|{\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}|}\right]&\begin{aligned} \text{if}~&|{\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}|\leq{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}~\text{and}\\
&{|1-\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}|\leq|\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}|}\end{aligned}\\
\displaystyle\frac{1}{2{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}}\left[1+\ln({\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}^{2}-|{\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}|^{2})+\frac{(1/3)}{{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}+|{\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}|}+\frac{(1/3)}{{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}-|{\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}|}\right]&\begin{aligned} \text{if}~&|{\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}|\leq{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}~\text{and}\\
&1\leq{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}-|{\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}|\end{aligned}\\
\displaystyle 1-(1/3)|{\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}|&\begin{aligned} \text{if}~&|{\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}|\geq{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}~\text{and}\\
&{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}+|{\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}|\leq 1\end{aligned}\\
\displaystyle\frac{1}{4{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}}\left[1-2(|{\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}|-{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}})+(1/3)(|{\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}|-{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}})^{2}+2\ln({\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}+|{\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}|)+\frac{(2/3)}{{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}+|{\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}|}\right]&\begin{aligned} \text{if}~&|{\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}|\geq{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}~\text{and}\\
&{|1-|\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}||\leq|\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}|}\end{aligned}\\
\displaystyle\frac{1}{2{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}}\left[\ln\frac{|{\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}|+{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}}{|{\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}|-{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}}+\frac{(1/3)}{|{\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}|+{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}}-\frac{(1/3)}{|{\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}|-{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}}\right]&\begin{aligned} \text{if}~&|{\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}|\geq{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}~\text{and}\\
&1\leq|{\mathnormal{U}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}|-{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}\end{aligned}\end{cases}.

(C11)

C.3
Emission and fluence

The effective isotropic energy is found using the (source-frame) effective luminosity and the (source-frame) chord crossing time:

\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}=\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}.

(C12)

The polarization, luminosity, and chord duration are all presumed independent, so the mean energy fluence is

{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}=\frac{|\mathsf{\Pi}_{\mathsf{x}}|}{2}{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}}\frac{\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}}{1+\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}{\left\langle|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}/\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}}.

(C13)

If the uniform drift rate distribution is adopted and the mean drift rate is near zero (
|{\mathnormal{\Delta}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}|\ll{\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{J}}}
),

{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\epsilon}}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}={\left\langle\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}}{\left\langle\mathnormal{\tau}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}\approx{\left\langle\mathnormal{\zeta}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}^{2}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}}\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}^{2}\cdot~\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{1-(1/6){\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}}{1+(1/2){\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}}&\text{if}~{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}\leq 1}\\
\displaystyle\frac{1}{2{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}}\frac{1+\ln{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}+(2/3){\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}^{-1}}{1+(1/2){\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}}}&\text{if}~{\overline{\mathnormal{u}}_{\mathsf{x};\mathrm{J}}\geq 1}\end{cases}.

(C14)

From this windowed energy, it is easy to find
{\left\langle\mathfrak{h}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}
(equation
91
):

{\left\langle\mathfrak{h}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}=\frac{|\mathsf{\Pi}_{\mathsf{x}}|}{2}{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}}{\left\langle{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}}{y}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}}\frac{\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}}{1+\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}{\left\langle|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}/\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}}.

(C15)

In the chord model, if
{\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{J}}}\gg\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}/\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}
, the typical broadcast in a sample is a high drift rate signal that slashes through the sample “box” from low to high frequency or vice versa. Fixing broadcast effective isotropic luminosity, these typical broadcasts are much fainter than the much slower drifting broadcasts with
\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}\approx 0
. Now, the mean total fluence is found by integrating the sampled drift rate distribution,

\frac{d\left\langle{\mathfrak{H}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle}{d\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}}=\frac{d\left\langle{\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle}{d\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}}{\left\langle\mathfrak{h}_{\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}}=\frac{|\mathsf{\Pi}_{\mathsf{x}}|}{2}{\left\langle\mathring{\mathnormal{\ell}}_{\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathrm{J}}}{\left\langle{{\chi}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathsf{x}|\mathrm{B}}}{y}_{\mathnormal{\epsilon};\mathrm{B}}\right\rangle_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}}{\mathnormal{Z}_{\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}{\left\langle\mathnormal{N}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\star}\right\rangle}\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}{\uppsi\left[\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathrm{J}}}.

(C16)

We find
\left\langle{\mathfrak{H}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}(|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|\leq\mathnormal{\delta})\right\rangle=\left\langle{\mathfrak{H}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle{{F\left[|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|\right]_{\mathrm{J}}}(\mathnormal{\delta})}
(note the use of the unbiased CDF). The fastest drifting half of the
population
contributes half the expected fluence. Yet this half is overrepresented in a sample. In the uniform drift rate model, if
|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|\gg\mathsf{B}_{\mathsf{x}}/\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{x}}
, the bottom quartile of
sampled
broadcasts in
|\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}|
contribute half the fluence.

As a result, the observed
{\mathfrak{H}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}
typically underestimates
\left\langle{\mathfrak{H}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle
because small samples tend to miss the slowest drifting broadcasts. For a uniform drift rate distribution, a few broadcasts suffice to counteract this effect. Biasing becomes more of an issue if the distribution has a long tail. For an exponential distribution
{\uppsi\left[\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}\right]_{\mathrm{J}}}=(1/{\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{J}}})\exp(-\mathnormal{\delta}_{\mathrm{B}}/{\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{J}}})
, the fraction of sampled broadcasts that contribute half the fluence falls to
(1-\ln 2)/2\approx 15\%
as
{{\overline{\mathnormal{\delta}}_{\mathrm{J}}}}\to\infty
. Thus, we might need to sample
\sim 5\text{--}10
broadcasts until
{\mathfrak{H}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}
starts converging to
\left\langle{\mathfrak{H}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle
. Heavy-tailed distributions like power laws would require still more broadcasts to be sampled before the aggregate emission converges to expectations from
\left\langle{\mathfrak{H}_{\mathsf{x}\mathchar 59\relax\mathrm{J}}^{\mathrm{B}}}\right\rangle
.
